The dress has quite a I Princess effect, but in reality there is a seam at got to do," the young officer remarked at last. tho waist on either side, Which is introduced to The two leant their elbows on the table, the one listening whilst the other talked; and when they parted there was an understand- ing between them which boded ill for the smuggling ringleader and his men. "There, that's off my chest !" Elliott observed when the other had left. "Now I'll sail into my future father-in-law, though, faith, I'd as soon stand in front of a double-shotted six-pounder. But never mind, it's for Madge—and I'd risk a lot more for her! " That the young lieutenant meant what he said no one may doubt, and yet the task he had taken upon himself—the disclosure of his love-story to Richard Mortimer,winding up with a request for the latter's benediction—required nearly all the fortitude, and certainly every atom of the self- control, he possessed. Mortimer was a man of vitriolic humour. Grimly silent, his long, sinuous fingers closely interlaced, his keen eyes fixed upon the man before him, lips upon which there hung the shadow of a sneer, Richard Mortimer sat and listened to the end, and when at last he spoke Jack found his hopes tumbled to the ground like a pack of cards. "I have no doubt, Lieutenant —er—Elliott, " Mortimer began, in razor-edged tones, "that I ought to feel deeply honoured by the proposal you have so elegantly conveyed to me ; but I regret to say I tun quite incapable of the appreciation that your case demands. lam delighted to hear of the confidence reposed in you by your superior officers; I am equally gratified by the assurance of . l eto ' which

lips abeot. It's hurtin' me badly this talkin', but ah 'nun gang on till Vend. "Yance ah'd a laal lass "—after another pause— I "an' she wer just the beet o' me life, an' me beet went oot. She wee bad a lanc, time, bit nut too long for Miss Mortimer to bide by her; she wes here mornin' noon an' neet, an' it wer yan o't' bonniest sights on God's earth to see her wid her angel ways makkin' my bairn's days brighter— an' her slippin' away easier. An' ah'd do owt to repay a wee bit o' me debt o' gratitude. "Noo she's gone ower t'oald rooml fur me. Why, man, deem's nowt wid her smoothin' yo're pillow, an' coolin' yo're burnin' broo, an' smilin' seah peaceful and,comfortin' like intil yo're face. "An' noo, to mek up for it a bit, I'se gene to leave her a legacy—a legacy o' joy, if yo're as good a man as ah tak' ye to be. Will ye oppen yon drawer?" Awe, fear, hope, curiosity—his brain in a whirl under the combined buffeting of all these emotions—the yowl.' officer obeyed the command. "There's a key ia'bottom." "Is this it ? " "Aye! Now oppen that corner cupboard." "Yes, what next ?" "Yo see that lad l oak box—wool, git them peppers oot an' read them." Noisily, with all its old rollicking haste, the little clock on the shelf ticks out the minutes, and when ten of them have gone, Jack Elliott, with whitened face and quivering limbs, looks upon that other white face on the pillow, and hoarsely gasps: "It's a lie, man—it's a lie! It cannot be true." "Pse a dyin' man, Meister Elliott —am I likely to lie ? " is the only response. Hastily Elliott picks up the papers and once more devours their contents; then, turning to the bed, he grips the smuggler's hand. "Yo believe noo ? " "I must. I cannot help it." "An' yo'll—yo'll—yo'll never tell her ? " "Galbraith," Elliott gravely answers, "haven't I told you that I love her ? "

Like some mysterious creature of the night, the Water Lily, all her lights extinguished, swept round the Mull; then her canvas was braced up, and a course set for the harbour in which the whisky barrels were to be secretly landed. Judged by the free-and-easy bearing of the blue- guernseyed men lounging about the deck the vessel might have been engaged upon a perfectly fair and square enterprise; but the thing that at so lightly upon them was not indifference, but habit, and, without any manifestation of the fact, every ear was tuned to catch the wandering whispers of the wind-swept waters, and every eye wide open for its sights. Prom Duddon Mouth to Morieambe the Water Lily revelled in a reputation gloriously her own. Not another lu,ger on the coast could shew such a clean pair of heels, and it was openly vaunted that she would never be taken so long as Tom Galbraith was entered on her log as skipper. Around Galbraith the years had naturally gathered a mantle of seafaring glory, and yet, as with lumbering roll he paces the little deck, while his craft bores its way through the night, his exterior presents nothing of heroic suggestive- ness. He is just a bushy-bearded, broad- shouldered, squat little sailorman, and it is only hit gls.s.ming eyes that reveal his alert intellect; to which ha owes his smuggling supremacy. Like the rest of his digs, moreover, he is utterly unconscious of any offence against the moral code underlying his profession, and on shore he is one of the most pious of mortals, and would sooner go without his Sunday's supper than his prayer-meeting. Thus the man with whose capture Jack Elliott has been entrusted. On reaching a point about a league from his goal the skipper grbwled a few words of com- mand, and a lantern was fished up the cuddy and held over the bows, and in a few seconds a - tly ,oint. Twice

Racked by the revelation that had come to him, hardly knowing what to think, uncertain as to what course to pursue, Elliott paced the Kittiwake's tiny deck and endeavoured to un- ravel the many threads in this woefully tangled skein. He was still there when the first flush of the autumn dawn fell like a benediction across the waters, but by this time he had got the thing well in hand, and the chaos had been reduced to something like order. One thing he felt to be imperative, he must see Madge's father again ; for the rest, events must be left to sort themselves out. His second reception was quite as chilling as the first had been. Mortimer was surprised by the visit, it is true, annoyed also, but the man was invested with a wonderful power of self- control, his features ever served him as a mask, and the look which he fixed upon his visitor was exactly as before, a combination of indifference and contempt. Concluding that Jack had called to renew his request for Zadge's hand, he deter- mined to cut the interview as short as possible. "I'm afraid, Mr. Elliott, that you are a person of poorer judgment than I had imagined. It will save your time, and mine, however, if I inform you at once that I am not in the habit of chang- ing my mind in the course of a week, and that even the brilliance of your smuggler-hunting has failed to impress me with a sense of your fitness 'as a son-in-law. tinder the circumstances--' "Steady, sir, steady ! THE INDEPENDENT ORDER OF

ODDFELLOWS.

Distribution of Prizes.

The first annual presentation of the silver challenge cup and medals given by P.P.G.M. Thomas S. Steel and other prizes won by members of the Constitution lodge in the bowling handicap held at the King and Queen Hotel on Saturday, 10th September took place at the Pelican Inn Buttermarket-street on Saturday evening. N.G. Richard K. Wilson presided, and there ' were else present—P.P.G.M. Thomas Steel, P.G.M. Lot Brooks of the Cadishesd Lodge, L.G.M. George , 11. NV. Steel, V.G. T. Ellison, E.S. Frederick B.Mellor, J.T. Joseph Grounds, P.S.C. Frederick Webster' P.G. 1 Holloway, D.G.M. Freeman. The prizes were distri- I buted by P.P.G.M. Thomas S. Steel and P.G.M. Lot Brookes. The proceedings took the form of a smoker and a very merry evening was spent. The Ca kniMAN after welcoming the district officers and brethren, said their first howling handicap had been a great success and they had a balance in band. Not only had P.P.G. M. Steel presented the Lodge with a silver cup and a gold medal for the winner but he con- sidered the play of the runner up was so praiseworthy that he had decided to giro him a gold centred silver medal in addition to his prize. He (the chairman) trusted that the cup would be the means of bringing the members together at many so,:ial functions in the year to come. (Applause.) P.P.G.M. STnnt, then presented the cup and rr edals and the Prow. Grand Master Lot Brookes distributed the other prizes to the winners as follows :-Ist prize, G. Holbrook; 2nd, T. G. Ford; 3rd, N. FIGHTING MEE DOGS.

At the B )rough Police Court on Monday, before the Mayor (presiding) and other magistrates, James Leonardy and Janes Hopkins were charged with lighting on the 3rd of the present month. Leonardy did not appear. William Hewitt, Friars Green, said that about 2.0 ia the afternoon he heard a row outside and on going Into the street saw the two meeflghting like dogs." Hopkins face was streaming with blood and he looked in a very pitiable ocndition. Hopkins said he was fighting in self-defence. He was fined 10s. and costs or 14 days and Leonardy for not appearing was fined ta)s. rod cc its or one month.

Mr. Frederick W. Stewart, of 42a, James-street, Devonport, is a staunch type of the Handyman. He has sailed under the Union Jack in all parts of the world, and has seen active service in the Zulu, Soudan, and Boer wars, and in many a bush light in the West Coast of Africa. He holds the Royal Humane Society's Medal for saving the life of a little child off the coast of Newfoundland. He is well known and highly respected in the Three Towns, and many local.

CLERGY, ATAGISTRATES, AND TOWN COUNCILLORS.,

are familiar with this remarkable case recorded here. "It was in 1901 I was first taken ill," he says. "It was nothing more than a throbbing pain in the sides and a weakness in the back—not bad enough to keep me away from work. But the strain of my duties soon told upon me, and at last I broke down completely. "The first doctor I called—l have had thireen in all —told me it was sciatica. He applied salt bags to the affected part,and that eased me for a time, but I quickly grow worse instead of better, and eventually had to keep to bed altogether. I was so helpless that I could not move in bed without assistance. My left leg was Lent up

ALL OUT OF SHAPE, and I completely lost the use of it. It was always cold and nothing would drive the sciatic pain away. "In 1904 I became in such a state that I had to be taken in a cab to the hospital. There I was kept on a strict milk diet, and the secretions from the kidneys were tested three timos every day. If I closed my eyes the nurses would shake me to rouse me ; they said they dared not let me fall off to sleep or I should never wake again. The doctors said it was impossible for roe to last long, and looked upon me as a freak of INature to be living at all seeing my condition. I was in such agony,that I. would have welcomed death,and I appealed to the physicians to let me die. POWERLESS AND DEFORMED.

Doctoring by Contract.—Mr. Kipling's picture of Miss Wren will, f course, soon take her place the doctor as a sort of hero, who always does his duty, in the small but highly - disciplined army of , 'o • no matter at what personal sacrifices to himself, will.be "medical women," of whom the oldest in ago appreciated by the doctor's wife. Meals kept waiting, and standing—that is. Dr. Elizabeth Blackwell— or hastily left half-finished, ctlls up the tube at night, has already celebrated her jubilee as physician. , and the necessity of being agreeable to people she A SMART EVERYDAY HAT. hates, because it does not do to make enemies, are but some of the trials that specially apply to the doctor's wife. But most general practitioners would • is a One has grown so accustomed to think of tho winter hats as mammoth creations that one rather be too busy than have too little work, and in 1 little apt to overlook the smaller chapeaux for America they are complaining of the competition of everyday wear. And yet there are a great many a new sort of medical Trust, which, in return for the ; charming small models for regular use, which payment of a small annual charge,undertakes to furnish i *re quite as becoming as and very much inoro medical attendance and treatment whenever required. useful than the huge hat of more dressy wear. There is nothing new in the idea of paying an annual A particularly attractive little everyday hat is fee to the doctor, whether you require his services or pictured in our sketch. The hat itself is a not, but it is only lately that the custom has become very popular with the public in America, and is not unlikely to extend to this country. Fan Painting.—Artistic work, by which many girls strive to add alittle to their dress allowance, is not as a rule very profitable. Time was when a very ordinary all-round teacher sufficed for the whole of a girls education, but nowadays every branch of study is specialised, and artistic work of every kind is taught in the schools and colleges. Consequently in the kind of work turned out by amateurs, the supply greatly exceeds the demand, but a few succeed very well at hand-painting fan work. The plant necessary is by no means expensive, but the amateur artist usually finds some difficulty in working open the strened silk gauze, to which too damp a wash is fatal, and re-touching spoils the work. An important point is the selection of a suitable design, and if the amateur aspires to make something more than a hobby of this work it is best to have a few lessons from artists who make a speciality of instruction in fan painting. Decorations and Furnishing.—When taking into consideration the decoration and furnishing of a room a capital plan is to follow closely in Nature's footsteps. For example, if a yellow and green scheme is conterup- plated, a daffodil offers the most exquisite shades, and though yellow is perhaps a little out of fashion just now, the colour is really one of the best to choose, especially for small and somewhat dark rooms. It is expanding in effect, and moreover gives illusion of sun- light on the most murky days. Blue and green is,at the moment, a combination selected by many housewives, famed for their artistic rooms, the bright blue of the forget-me-not and the dull green of its leaves, being the tints usually decided upon. Black woodwork has been chosen by two recent brides, the walls being painted white or a deep orange. Net Blouses.—The silk blouse is little in request among "smart " women, its place being taken by the crepe de chine, or plain ivory washing net varieties. Elaborately tucked and inserted with a little real lace, few afternoon blouses look more dainty and smarter than those of ivory net, always provided the net is really fine, and the blouse is lined with the same fabric, and then drawn over a cream satin slip. It is really n arvellous how long these net blouses will last if washed and ironed at home, rni if a little simple Valenciennes is utilised the home worker will certainly net find them expensive. Folds better than Frills. The craze for the high, pleated collar frill goes merrily on, but without doubt the majority of us would do better to pin our faith to two or three folded bands of crepe, wherewith to finish off the top of our collars. Very slender women, with tiny necks, may be able to wear the frills,but even so,it will certainly be found that the bins folds give a better effect. Great care and pains should be taken by the amateur dressmaker to have her collars very high, and very carefully supported, otherwise the blouse, however pretty, will present a dowdy, and " ready-to-wear " a;pearance. WAITING FOR THE END.

"A second time I was taken to P-- Convales- cent Home, but growing no better, I pleaded to be allowed to return to my own home. The physician remarked to the nurse that I might just as well end my days at home as there, so I was wrapped in blankets and taken back in a conveyance. " During my long suffering I had been trying various kinds of advertised medicines, and now—at what seemed to be the end—l hoped against hope, and asked my wife to get some of Dose's backache kidney pills for me to try. The chemist did his best to persuade her to have his own backache ' pills instead of Doan's ; they were cheaper and just as good, he said. But my wife replied that she knew I would use nothing but Doan's, and she got Doan's. " The first box did me no good that I Could tell or, hot I did not expect miracles, £11:1C1 was not discouraged. I kept on with the pills, and when I was half-way through the second box I began to pass water in amazing quantities. I must have passed gallons a day. There was a never-ending desire to urinate. The relief this gave me was wonderful. I used to measure my swollen limbs every day with a little tape measure, and day by day I could see a distinct REDUCTION OF THE DROPSICAL SWELLINGS.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Scottish National Emblem.—We all know that the thistle is the national emblem of Scotland, but how it came to be so is a matter as to which accounts vary Miss Simpson, in her entertaining book " Folk Lore in Lowland ' Scotland," published by Messrs. Dent gives what is at least a very plausible version of its origin. This is to the effect that the invading Danes had stolen I a march on the sleeping Scots, but the thistle formed a sort of barbed wire entanglement round the Scottish camp, and the sharp pricks compelled the bare-legged Danes to give tongue. The Scots heard their smothered cruses, awoke and armed. That accord- inr, to Miss Simpson, is how the thistle came to be adopted as Scotland's insignia, with its defiant motto, "Ye daurna meddle wi' me "—or as it is given in Latin on the Order of the Thistle " Memo me impune lacess it." No one annoys me with impunity. Buckingham Palace.—lt often happens that the plain looking building is the more comfortable inside, but this is not the case with Buckingham Palace, which has the reputation of being the ugliest Royal residence in Europe. William IV. refused to live there, and our late Queen had such a dislike to the place that she never spent more than two or three weeks under its roof in the year. during the latter half of her reign. It is suggested that the Palace should be rebuilt, as in a measure it spoils the background to the Queen Victoria Memorial. Originally Goring House, and acquired by the Duke of i Buckingham, n 1698, it hBs been rebuilt and nmovated on several occasions. An immense sum was spent on the Palace shortly after Queen Victoria's Coronation, end what it has cost altogether would have sM:iced to build another Versailles, if the money had been applied to such a purpose. A BATCH OF POLICE ASSAULTS.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Joseph Williams, of Bowes-street, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Buttermarket-street and also with assaulting P.C. Owen Jones on Saturday night.—P.C. Edwards stated that he saw the prisoner in Buttermarket-street and he was creating a disturbance. He asked him to go away but he refused, and continued the disturbance, so that witness was compelled to take him into custody. He then became very violent end threw himself upon the ground. P.C. Owen Jones ' came to witness's assistance and received a couple of kicks under the chin. While prisoner was being placed in the calls by P.C. Leonard he bit the officer on the finger, causing it to swell.—He was sent to gaol for six weeks with hard labour. John Robinson, of Portland-street, was charged with , assaulting P.C. Owen Jones while he was taking the last ' prisoner to the Police Station. The evidence showed that prisoner rushed at P.C. Jones and struck him in the face, and then ran away. Ile was afterwards appre- hended. He was fined 205. and costs or one months' imprisonment. At the Occeaional Court, on Monday, three Irishmen, named John Moore, Michael Moore, and Thomas Burrows, were charged with having been drunk and dis- orderly at Great Sankey on Sunday night, and also with assaulting P.C. Corlett.—The evidence showed that when Corlett was taking John Moore to the lock-up for being drunk, he was set upon by the other prisoners, who kick( i him about the body.—They were fined 20s. each or one month's imprisonment. At the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday, John Hickmr Dakin's-yard, was charged with assaulting Police Constable Rylatt in Tanner's-lane the previous night. The officer was engaged in taking a woman into custody when the prisoner rushed at him and struck him a violent blow in the face. He afterwards ran away, but was arrested at his home later in the evening. —The Chief Constable remarked that this was the fifth assault on the police within eight days. The magistrates sent prisoner to gaol for six weeks with hard labour.

MR. SEDDON, M.P., AND SIR C. FURNESS. At a meeting of the Wigan Independent Labour Party on Sunday evening, Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., said that from what he had read of Sir Christopher Furness's offer to permit workmen to become shareholders with the great northern shipbuilding firm it struck him as being on a par with the nursery rhyme, "Will you come into my parlour, said the spider to the fly."

A public meeting convened by the Mayor was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, on Tuesday evening, to consider the formation of a Civic League of Help for Warrington. The central idea aimed at is the awakening of a civic and communal conscience with respect to the existence of preventable poverty and misery, and the establishment of a system of corarnuntl action for the purpose of removing or alleviating the same. If a League is formed for Warrington, it will seek to co-operate with all existing charitable agencies in the town. On ar count of the existence of so many of these already, and the difficulty of administering a Central Relief Fund wiihout falling into the very risk of overlapping and waste it is sought to avoid, it is not proposed that the League shall be a Relief Society. It will seek, rather, to secure the hearty co-operation of all existing agencies of the kind in the town ; it will issue to its district heads and helpers a directory containing the names, addresses, and objects of all such agencies ; it will remit cases requiring material or medical assistance to the agencies best fitted to deal with them ; it will seek to prevent the great evil of overlapping by developing the idea of a Central Clearing-house for the operations of all such agencies. The hope is entertained that cases of a special character not provided for by any existing agency, may be placed under the care of such private persons as may be found willing and able to undertake them. The Mavort presided over the meeting and there was a large and representative attendance, including several of the clergy of the town. Mr. A. W. PERLUS, who has taken the leading part in bringing the movement to the fore in Warrington, read numerotss letters of apology for non-attendance. Mr.C. E. Parker wrote :—" Dear Mr. Mayor,—l am in receipt of your circular in connection with the Civic League of help. The ideas are excellent, and a society working on the lines indicated has great possibilities of usefulness. My only fear in Warrington is whether there are a sufficient number in the borough who, as you so well put it,are willing that all class distinctions should be forgotten, every party label laid aside, all differences of creed or polity lost sight of. If there are a sufficient number who will rise to this ideal, then the League of Help may become an instrument of very great good. If, however, after thoroughly testing the question by your meeting, or otherwise, you And that that the league does not enlist a sufficient number of representatives of all classes of thought and creed it would, in my opinion' be a mistake to go on with it. If the league only enlists the sympathies and help of one section, end is not thoroughly representative, it will , simply divert useful energies from other spheres of activity and not accomplish its end, in which case it would, in my humble opinion,be wise for the promoters to abandon the scheme." . .

The Rev. W. Bracecamp in the course of his letter wrote:—" There is a feeling abroad that the league would interfere with other charities' which are doing such an excellent work. Instead of this being the case I feel sure it would be a real help to them, because each case would be investigated, and then if found deserving it would be recommended to any existing charity. If nothing could be done then this league would try and do something. There is too much indiscriminate giving which does far more harm than good. This league would prevent this if every person would take it up and give it his hearty support. I wish the meeting every success." The Rev. J. F'. Matthews wrote :—" Dear Mr. Mayor, —I desire to express in the warmest terms my sympathy with the objects of the Guild of Help. Although not able to be present at the meeting which you have called for this evening, I hope to do all I can to further its interests, if the result of your gathering takes practical shape. I was fortunate in being present at the inaugura- tion of the one formed in Manchester about two years ago, and of all the institutions formed within recent years for the co-ordination and unification of all kinds of philanthropy, the Cuild of League of Help seems to me by far the best If for once citizens can put aside their various isms ' and other prejudices,' and unite for the sake of men' in a crusade of active love, the man in the street will soon come to believe in the reality of our religious professions." Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins wrote:—" lam inclined to think that there is considerable force in the remarks which I have seen in the public Press during the past few days, commenting upon this :tatter, to the effect that if the various institutions for good work were supported as fully as they might be by our citizens there would be little or no necessity for a league of this kind, for the purpose of awakening the civic and communal conscience' to the existence of preventable poverty in our midst and for removing or alleviating the same. May I con- cisely draw your attention to what has been done by the Committee of the Warrington Children'sl Summer camp during the past few years : —lnaugurated, 1903 ; permanent barnp established at Prestatyn, 1906 ; number of children sent in six years, 2,300 ; number of children sent during 190 S, 400. Adults and their families are accommodated during the time the children are not in camp. About 200 persons from Warrington have been in camp during the past season. There is every indication of further and healthy develop- ment in this direction. If an institution of this kind is supported to the extent it might be, without making any great call upon the resourees of society,a very great deal more good work might be done in the direction indicated in the circular letter convening the meeting. The original idea, coupled with the formation of the children's camp, i.e., the establishment of a permanent convalescent home for the benefit of the poorer citizens of Warrington, might be developed and established at comparatively little cost, Letters had also been received from the Revs. Canon Stevenson and John Yonge Mrs. Garnett, Mr. A. H. Crosield, M.P., and Mr. Ve. Peter Rylands. The Mayon said Mr. Parker's letter was a very wise and ;judicious one, for unless they could enlist the sympathy of the whole of the town the League could not be worked. They must unite all sections, and all religions feeling must be put on one side. There must be no class legislation, but a spontaneous feeling of love and fellowship towards their neighbours. Mr. R. C. PHILLIrs, of Manchester, then spoke of the objects of the League of Help. He pointed out that the Leagues already established in England differed from the Elberfeld :system of relief in that they did not receive and distribute funds on their own account. They were advisory bodies. The towns were divided into districts and each was managed by a small com- mittee of helpers under a head. Cases were investigated and certain steps recommended, but the League did not interfere with the work of the existing charitable institutions. These Leagues of Help originated only a few years ago in England. The first one was at Brad- ford,and shortly afterwards Halifax followed, and many others succeeded until there were now between 30 and 10 in existence. The function of the League was primarily to assist those out of work,or who were sick, or who had met with sonic unforeseen disaster, and, if possible, they saved the home from being broken upend the people from being sent to the workhouse. If they found it was best to apply to the Guardians, they advised that being done. The speaker showed how charitable organizations could be imposed upon under the exist- ing system where there was no central advisory authority. Overlapping of charity was a thing which should be discouraged, as it was a most serious affair in some districts: it ought to be strenuously guarded against. In Manchester one-third or one-haif of the city was laid out for the work of the league. Cards were displayed in the uindows containing the names of the persons interested in the league in the different districts, and if a policeman or anyone else heard of a case of distreks he communicated with one or other of the committee and steps were taken to relieve distress. The work in Manchester was a success, and during the past eleven months, although the league was not very well-known, no fewer than 1,233 cases had been dealt with. There were 500 helpers at work in different parts of the city. He would be glad indeed if Warrington found it could advantageously follow the lead of so many other towns in England in this matter. (Applause.) The Rev. J. RENSTEAW BATLEY, of Eccles, also delivered an interesting address. He said chronic: poverty was a blot on our national flag which it uas our bounden duty to try and remove. No foreign nation could bairn us as we could harm ourselvasand no foreign people could insult vs as we could permit ourselves to be insulted by the condition of things that existed in a chronic manner in our own beloved and wealthy England. Replying to those who said that nothing could be done 51 the matter and that tho problem of poverty must ale rya remain unsolved, the speaker said the city of Elberfeld by is system which was on somewhat similar lines to that proposed by the League had solved the problem of chronic poverty. Of all the curses 1 under which the modern world rested, especially the large towns, he thought private charity was one of the most grievous. People gave simply to relieve their conscience and not because they had carefully satisfied themselves as to the deserving nature of the case to be assisted. If only all the money that was given away indiscriminately, and did untold mischief and perpetuated the evil they were trying to eradicate, were clubbed together and put in the hands of such a guild as they were thinking of forming in Warrington, it would more than do all that was required to be done in any given area, and leave a surplus for working expenses. (Applause.) He wanted to dissociate the whole thing from the Church although he admitted that the Church must find the inspiration for the movement and largely work hnd help. Let the churches realise that it was not for them to keep their doors open and their peas filled by doles of blankets and other good things, but that it was their's to help along with their neighbours and friends of all denominations and of none, to solve this great problem. The speaker scathingly criticised the Poor Law system and said he looked for great things from the Cornmiesion on the Poor Law and from the fact that some of our present rulers end governors had been over in Germany and hed studed Poor Law administration there. In many matters of social reform we in England were a long way behind our neighbours on the Continent. The Poor Law did nothing to prevent a man frcni going down the hill. It only got at him when he was at the very bottom. The league was not, as had been stated by serce, " a glorified charity organization." There was no continuity of policy in a charity oorganiza-

(Applause). The Rev. E. C E. CATILET3N said the work done by the churches and chapels in relieving poverty was in his opinion very properly restricted to their own mem- bers who had fallen into nee' or come under some calamity,and the roller thus distributed found Its way to cases that would never be likely to COMB under the notice of a central body like the League of Help. There was much less overlapping in the matter than might generally be supposed because each clergyman or minister as a rule looked after his own flock and a parti- cular district. There were those for whom the churches were not responsible—the casuals who came into the town for a short time and never came into touch with a religions body and the thriftless individuals whose need had perhaps been brought on by themselves. If there was an organization to deal specially with those cases and to be consulted in matters of don')t he believed it would be welcomed by the clergy and ministers and charitable workers. Miss BnoannENT contended that it was a great mistake for the churches and chapels to be looked upon largely as relief agencies. That ought not to be their work. Many people went to churches and Sunday schools for what they could get out of them. If the clergy and ministers kept to their own spiritual duties there would not he half the pauperism that now existed amongst them. Speaking as a member of the Board of Guardians, she could assure the audience that they were very chary about helping anyone who had been recom- mended by the clergy—(laughter)'— who were very much led away by the kindness of their hearts. Mr. W. NIGNTING.LLE in supporting the proposal to form a League said he knew the ministers of the town were often completely taken in. (Laughter.) Alderman BENNETT said he hoped some organization like the League would be established. He always felt that it was an absolute reproach and disgrace to our civilization that such an appalling amount of suffering and poverty should exist amongst us. The league would, he was sure, do something to ameliorate the present conditions if it got the necessary help but his one doubt was whether, in these days of football and pleasure, there would be the necessary number of people ready to devote themselves to the service of their The Rev. J. IZENSILIW B win- in reply said the League did not want to interfere with the sacred relations between a church and its own members. Their desire was to stop indiscriminate charity. The Rev. C. lIARVEV COOK, in proposing a vote of thanks to the speakers, said the idea of the League was exactly what the title suggested. It, was a civic help. That was, " I am my brother's keeper, but my brother is my keeper. He is one of the members of the borough ; so am I." They could not understand that men and women could live amongst their fellow beings without feeling that they had a social responsibility. They asked for service, for counsel, sympathy and help in many ways, other than mere monetary help. They wanted money, of course,if the scheme were carried for- ward, but more than that they wanted self-sacrilice for the good of the citizen. (Applause.) Mr. J. S. Ilmr, seconded, and said there should be no difficulty in forming a guild in ‘Varrington. The resolution was heartily carried, and after some discussion the following Provisional Committee was appointed to report on the scheme at a future meeting to be called, viz., the Rev. C. Harvey Cook, the Rev. W. 13racecamp, Miss Broadbent ; Messrs. A.AVV. Perris, J. S. Hill, W. Mason and E. Osborne.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 17, 1908.

CIVIC LEACUE OF HELP.

SUGGESTED NEW DEANIZATION

FOR WARRINGTON.

tion society, and that was what he objected to. It never laid hold of the popular imagination, and people generally regarded it as the very pretty fad of a hand- ful of people who had not got much to do. That was unfair, he Icnew. Oa the other hand the Civic League of Help appealed to everyone. It was not a private fad, as it was inaugurated and foulded in the Council Chamber, with the Atyor in the claair. Tne problem of poverty in any community was not the problem of individuals or of the churches, or of a small society. It was the problem of the community. (Haar, heir, and applause.) An eicient system nalant an economical system, and a great deal was wasted by want of organiza- tion. He wanted to sea begging from door to door done away with, as it had bna at Elberfeld. (Applause.) He hoped the work would be successful in Warrington, as it had been in Eccles, and that it would reduce the poor rate, drive away begging from door to door, and prevent malingering. Let them cultivate the civic spirit and take for their motto the noble words of Dr. Paton, of Nottingham, " To love is to serve, And to serve is to save."

FASHION AND FAT.

When fashion demands slender gracefulness of women who have to break corset laces to make them- selves even presentable, isn't it fortunate that science steps in and makes their task easy? For dieting and exercising science has substituted a pleasant, wholesome mixture that can be obtained from any chemist, viz : One half-ounce of garmola, one ounce of fluid extract of Glycyrrhiza 8.F., one ounce of pure Glycerine 8.P., and Peppermint Water to make six ounces in all. One dessertspoonful of this mixture after meals and at bedtime will take off four to seven pounds of fat a week without causing wrinkles, and without interfer- ing with the diet or distressing the stomach in the slightest degree.

STOCKTON HEATH.

PRESZSTITION.—Mr. J. Hallows, the teacher of St. Thomas's men's class (section B), was on Sunday the I 'recipient of a gift from the members, in the shape of a silver-mounted writing case. Mr. Rushton, on behalf of himself and the other members of the class, asked Mr. Hallows to accept the case as a token of their regard and appreciation of his work in comic lition with the class. Mr Hallows in reply, said the presentation lidi come to him as a pleasant surprise as he had not the least intimation of it. He should ever prize the case as a gift from his young men, but he valued the spirit that prompted it ten times more. It told him as plain as words could tell him that he bad won the sympathy of the ears. No one knew better than them- selves the uphill work he had had slue he took the class over, but of late he had been cheered time after time by their kind consideration and assistance, and he took that beautiful gift as a pledge that henceforth Ps teacher and members, they were going to stand shoulder to shoulder for the help of each other and by all means possible to carry forward the work of Christ and His Church in the parish. (Applause.)

CURRANTS AND BANANA COOKING COMPETITIONS.

, An interesting series of home-cooking competitions open to the United Kingdom, and with prizes of the value of £1,500, has been arranged. Professional bakers and chefs are barred, and each county will constitute a competition district, the value of prizes in each being varied according to the number of entries. What competitors have to do is to prepare and cook some tasty pastries, puddings, cakes, buns, scones, preserves, or any other dainties which contain a good proportion of currants or bananas, or currants and bananas com- bined. No entrance fee is charged, as the objects of the competition are to promote the daily use of these wholesome and nutritious fruits and to encourage home cooking. On completion of the judging, all the articles sent in for competition will be distributed free among institutions providing for the poor. Full particulars as to the conditions of the competition, which closes at the end of the present month, can be obtained by writing to "Currants," 231, Strand, London' who will not only forward entry forms, but a useful booklet of recipes of tasty dishes which can be made by the use of currants and bananas. Readers who are communicat- ng in this way are requested to mention the Warrington Examiner."

PRESENTATION TO MR. AND MRS. CHURCEITLL Mr. Winston Churchill, M.P., President of the Board of Trade, and his wife were on Wednesday evening the recipients of two handsome presents subscribed to by the Libsrals of Manchester. The presentation to Mr. Churchill was made at Belle Vue, in the presence of a crowded gathering, that to Mr. Churchill being a large silver ce.ntre-piece, while Mrs. Churchill received a diamond pendant and bracelet. Mr. Churchill, in acknowledging the gifts, said that the influence of Manchester had been the turning-point in his political life. Alluding to the cotton dispute, he said he had refrained from approaching either party, lacz.arse he believed the good common sense, patriotism, and honour of Lancashire folks would, with patience, bring about a reasonable and honest settlement of this melancholy and disastrous business, lie also hinted that a sliding- scale might do something to make the Brooklands agreement immune from being marred by periodical I rials of this sort.

Mr. F. J. .A.4rtirner has taken a bold sten in arrang- ing, at the littTe galleries of" rue Amateur Photographer, an exhibition of rejected pictures. About 153 prints are hung, and of these probably 110 were rejected at the Salon, while the rest are selected from those refused at the Royal. Of course, there are different opinions about many of the individual pictures even in this " rejected " show, bit I think th it even the most critical will agree that from them 11 to 53 might be selected which would greatly add to the strength and interest of the Photographic Salon, if they ware included with the pictures already hung there. Many would contend that the Salon would not, be weakened even if a few of the pictures on its walls were obliged to be removed in order to make room for a few of those which have been , refused. In the collection of rejecteds, the main room has a limit of not more than thrPs prints by any one worker, and we flnd flue examples by Ward Muir, C. \Ville, Harold Baker, Frederick 11. AUTOCEIROME LSCTURES AT THE A great many provincial photographers seem to be unaware that the reduced-price tickets in the Red Book are available for the nights when the lectures on " The Thames from Source to Sea " are being given at the New Gallery. NATIONAL PHOTOGRAPHIC PORTRAIT GALLERY.

Although I have devoted a good deal of space in this column to the R.P.S. Exhibition, I have not nearly exhausted the interesting items. In the Gallery around the Central Hall one wall is devoted to an impressive collection of portraits of eminent British subjects. This collection was suggested by Mr. Frederick flollyer a couple of years ago, and has bean under the considera- tion of the Council of the Royal Photographic Society and of a Portrait Gallery Committee appointed by the Council. The collection consists of pormanent prints of portraits of distinguished persons, which may be accepted at any time, but which will not be added to the Gallery until at least twelve months after the death of the subject. The selection shown at the exhibition consists of 120 pictures, of which roughly equal quanti- ties are, contributed by Mr. Frederick Hollyer, P.S., and Mr. G. C. \Vhitfisld. Three are from negatives by Mrs. Julia Margaret Cameron, and one very fine portrait of the King comes from the Swan Electric Engraving Company. The inclusion of the King's portrait is the only exception to elle rule that the pictures must represent deceased celebrities.

WHY NOT TRY IT? A story is told of a bashful lover who expressed to his sweetheart his wonder whether she would seriously resent it if he should steal a kiss. To this the maiden is said to have replied "Just you try it." and the story goes to the effect that the bashful lover never did, for he was not quite sure as to the maiden's meaning. lam reminded of this failure to experiment by some of the questions that come from my readers. For instance, some time back I gave a formula for bleaching out the image of a silver print, and a little while ago a reader took the trouble to write me a letter, to use a penny stamp for its postage, and to enclose another penny stamp for my reply, for the purpose of telling me that he had made up the solution according to the formula and wanted to know how long he should leave the print in the bleach. He might have saved himself the trouble of writing, and answered his question in about live seconds if had simply put a little piece of print into the solution. One of the greatest hindrances to the pro- gress of many amateurs is thei! apparent inability to suggest the most simple experiment i for themselves. Probably half the questions which are addressed to photographic journalists could be answered by ten cainites devoted to very simple tests which ought to be obvious to every one. LONG EXPOSURES FOR STREET SCENES.

Every suggestion which will tend to keep our cameras in use during the winter instead of being put away ou dusty shelves is worth giving. As the light decreases many people think that street scenes are impossible because an exposure sufficient to give the buildings satisfactorily will be too long for some of the moving figures, with the result that they will be blurred. If the photographer is willing to do without the figures, the possibilities of street photography with long exposures are most surprising. trany of the best street views shown by Captain Gladstone and by Frederick H. Evans in the heyday of their lantern-slide activity were made by giving long exposures with the smallest possible stop, and with the result that the many moving figures left no impression what- ever upon the plate. An example of this kind of thing is to be seen in one of Frederick 11. Evans pictures in the Salon des Refuses, and the same picture is reproduced " Photographs of the Year," 1938. It is a view of the house of Joan of Arc, and was made on market morning. An extremely small stop was used with an exposure of two or three minutes. The result ws.s that although hundreds of people were passing to and fro in the field of the lens, none of them has affected the picture at all. Photographers often forget that a moving object which merely absorbs part of the light has practically no effect on the plate. It is only when a brilliantly lighted object passes across some portion that is otherwise in shadow that the plate is affected. When an exposure of two or three minutes is given, even the slowest sauntering people do not stay long enough in one position to make any impression.

MANCHESTER POULTRY AND PIGEON SHOW. The tenth annual show of prize poultry, pigeons and appliances is to be held at Belle Yue Gardens, on Tuesday, Wednesday and Thnrsday next, October 200, 121st and 22ad. The show now ranks as second to none in its clasp, and this year nearly 5,00 entries have been received. Many of the leading appliance manufacturers are represented, and the show is of considerable interest to the layman as well as the fancier. It is but once a year that the local public are afforded the opportunity of seeing this magnificent collection. Incubators are shown in the process of hatching chickens, in addition to many other interesting appliances. _

The 33rd annual report was read by the secretary (Mr. Plinston). It stated that the past year had had its bright as well its darker side. Some of the hopes and expectations experienced in their last issue bad not been realized. The apathy of several of the Ciliated branches and their delegates was to some extent respon- sikle for that. He was convinced that so soon as the Trade Union organisations in the town realised the importance of joint action and showed that amount of interest which was necessary to deserve success, then and not till then would their hopes be realised. Their last report showed 23 affiliated branches. Since then two—the engineers' and bakers' societies—had seceded. Four societies however had joined their ranks viz, the caretakers, Prudential agents, Royal Liver agents and Musicians, so that they had 23 unions connected with the council. Further additions were shortly anticipated and steps would be taken, he trusted with the earnest co-operation of every delegate, to induce the outside organisations to join them in their work. It was a pleasing duty to the report that the cash balance had been largely increased and stood higher than for many years. Among the many subjects that had called for attention were mentioned those of national housing municipal coal supply, old age pensions, and postal grievances. The Council in conjunction with the local I.L.P. organised a demonstration upon the important subject of old age pensions which was addressed by Messrs. G. N. Barnes and S. Walsh, M.P.'s. They rejoiced that pensions had been granted by the Government, and accepted such legislation, as the initial stage, believing that the principle was a right one. It was the duty of organised labour to demand a reduction in the age limit,and the removal of some of the obnoxious clauses. The President of the Council Mr. Richardson, together with Mr. Plumb were deputed to attend a conference upon the topic of National Housing, and gave an excellent report thereon, pointing out the necessity for legislation. Their Executive Committee had the matter in band with a view to local action if necessary. It was with sincere regret that they could "not record commercial prosperity. Unfortunately trade was bad—exceptionally so in some parts of the country —and although they were not suffering so severly as many Lancashire towns, they could not be unmindful of the seriousness of the outlook. The great

PROBLEM OF L'NEMPLOYMEN'4 I was still unsolved and was more acute than for several years past, thousands of voices cried out for work, yea pleaded for the right to exist. Misery and starvation was the lot of vast numbers of their brethren. Surely the time bad come when organised labour must demand a remedy. Charity might momentarily relieve the acute suffering but it was not the cure; the right to work must be insisted upon. It was sincerely to be regretted that strikes and lockouts had been so prevalent during the past twelve months. Especially had this been 93 amongst railway, shipbuilding an 4 cotton workers. The Trade Union Congress as the mouthpiece of Trade Unionism had called for conciliation and arbitration, believing that the best interests of all conceraed could be sesved by the adoption of such methods. It was cause for regret that the employers did not unitedly voice the same sentiment. They were promised legislation upon both those important subjects, but alas what a long delay! Mr. Winston Churchill's suegested scheme of a court of arbitration was acceptable as a temporary palliative with respect to trade dispute. In regard to postal grievances, the Executive of the Council were thoroughly investigating the details—i.e. -- terms to local postmen which were now in operation as a result of the " Flobhouse " commision. They ventured to say that the present terms were not only uncalled for, but absolutely unjust; and every effort would be put forth to remedy the same. The value of the labour representation had been strongly illustrated during the year just past and much useful work had heen done in Committee and Town Council by Councillors Fred Stott and J. Ball. With full know- ledge of the condition of the workers they had been able to look after their interests and that had been done earnestly and honestly, but they much required additional support. They were pleased to report that most of the branches had agreed to active work by the Trades Council in that direction and although time was too short for action this year, they contemplated putting in opnration at an early date the powers thus conferred. The secretary as the Councils' representative upon the education authority in submitting his fifth annual report, especially mentioned the important work re the feeding and medical inspection of school children, and as a member of those sub-ccmmittees pledged himself to strive for the proper working of both schemes. They sincerely regretted that during the year they had been deprived of the valuable services of a worthy comrade in the person of Councillor Fred Stott. They missed his advice in the Council's deliberation, but still more the town had lost a valuable councillor. His words of wise counsel at their August meeting would be remembered. As expressed in their testimonial letter to him they wished him many years of health and pros- perity. The Council desired to gain the active sympathy and support of all trade unionists during the coming year. Not only did they do active propaganda work, I - but their duty was to propound a forward policy. Many grievances demanded redress. Their work should not only be demonstrative but constructive. Trades unionism might be slumbering, but it was not dead. its clays of usefulness were not past; there was much work to be done. Their cause was just, their principles right ; let one and all determine to fight for their maintenance. If the workers were to be emancipated they must combine. They must cast aside the old shibboleths, and by concerted political action, remove the shackels that bound them. (Applause). A hearty Tote of thanks was accorded to the Secretary for his report.

RATS AND MICE.

HARMLESS TO OTHER ANIMALS.

The House of Commons on CLAUSE TWO. Wednesday night began the

discussion of Clause 11. Of the Licensing Bill, and they will be engaged on the details of the Bill for 601f13 weeks to coma. As Mr. ASQUITH and other members of the Government have told us it is a measure on which they are prepared to stake their reputation. No Government ever started out on licensing legis- lation with the idea that ir would increase their popularity, for the drink traffic is very powerful. Lord ROSEBERY once told us that if the State did not throttle the drink traffic the drink traffic would throttle the State. It is in the interests of the State that the Government have brought forward this Bill. In tha days of the last Government the brewers, who, for reasons of their own, had become possessed of the public-houses in the country, made a bargain with their friends, who were then in power, and secured for themselves privileges which ought never to have been granted. The central feature of the Act 1904 was that the State parted with many of its rights to the brewers. The central feature of Mr. Asuumfs Bill is the resumption by the State, after a fair interval, of rights with which it never ought to have parted. There will doubtless be many alterations in the Bill before it gets through the House of Commons, but the Government are bound to stand or fall by its central feature, which is State control of the drink traffic. .

In their declarations against the Bill,the brewers and their friends say very little about this part of the measure, although to them, 4 to the Govern-

ment, it is the most important of all its provisions. They do, it is true, shout about confiscation, and that sort of thing, and tell us that the Government are frittering away the value of the shares, which' seem, according to their story, to be largely held by "widows and orphans." The truth is, that the shares were going down in value at a great rate before ever the Licensing Bill was introduced, and that the rate of depreciation has not been acceler- ated at all by the measure. They are falling because the investing public are finding out that they were not worth what they were supposed to be worth, that the competition of brewer v. brewer for the possession of licensed houses artificially inflated their value, that many of the brewery companies which the brewers floated upon the unsuspecting "widows and orphans" were over-capitalised, and that, as Sir THOMAS WHITTAKER has shown, many of them have no proper provision for depreciation. Fourteen years would enable the sound concerns to do all that is necessary, almost without feeling it, and it is surely no business of the State to bolster up con- cerns which are not sound. But the Government have already shown that they are not wedded to fourteen years as the time limit if that can be shown to be unfair. All that they are concerned about is that at some period, not toe remote, the State shall resume full control.

The particular clause of the Bill under con- sideration on Wednesdsy provides for local option with regard to new licences. Is does not provide for general local option, but only that the inhabitants of any tlistrict shall have the right to say whether or not a new public-house shall be opened in their midst. There is no valid argu merit against such a proposal. Many of those who argued against it, and voted against it, are wealthy enough to select their own place of residence, and it may be taken as a certainty that they do not care to live too near a public- house. There are many districts in this country where a single individual owns the land for miles round, and has the absolute right to say whethe r a licensed house shall exist within his domain or sot. If it be tyranny to allow a majority of the inhabitants of any district to veto the opening of a drink shop in their midst, by what name shall we characterise the action of a single landlord who deprives all the people of the inestimable advantage of a public-house ? If we understand aright the arguments used against this clause they mean that its enactment would place the power in the hands of a few fanatics. Some of the Opposition speakers last night said so. How a few fanatics are to command a majority of the inhabitants was not explained. The argument presupposes that minorities are more powerful than majorities. We doubt, however, whether the Opposition really in their hearts believe that the fanatics would rule. What they do know is that the people themselves would not be so likely as magistrates to grant new lisenses, and they do not desire to limit the power of the drink traffic in any way. It is much too useful an asset at election times.

The Warrington Civic CIVIC LEAGUE OF League of Help promises HELP. soon to be enrolled among

local institutions. The attendance at the preliminary meeting on Tuesday was encouraging and there were indications of a desire en the part of representatives of different shades of opinion in the town to work unitedly for the relief of poverty on systematic and well organized lines. In some quarters an attempt is being made to decry the proposal on the ground that it would interfere with existing organizations, but there is no such intention. The League will simply act as a central advisory body and will refer the various cases which arise to the agencies best fitted to deal with them. It will not administer funds on its own account and will make the fullest use of the machinery already set in operation by charitably dispossd people. There will thus be a co-ordination of all the societies which are seeking to mitigate poverty and suffering and the plan of placing each district in charge of a committee with its recog- nized head should workiadmirably: Indiscriminate charity with its consequent overlapping is not in the interests of the whole community and tends to encourage malingering. Each case should be carefully considered on its merits and in this direction the mass of information which such a body as the League of Help would be able to command—if it were well supported by the towns- people—would be invaluable. A provisional committee is now at work preparing a scheme to be submitted to a future meeting and we hope that when the members have completed their labours the League will be successfully launched on a career of usefulness.

Though there is not THE MUNICIPAL much prospect of an all- Einerroxs.—A FOUR- round fight on the 2nd LINE CANDIDATE, Of November, there are many points of interest in the contests which seem assured. The attitude of the Conservative party alone is w,orthy of consideration. They unblushingly introduce another publican candidate,although the Trade has six devoted adherents in the Colwell already. Fortunately there does not seem much likelihood of the Orford electors being so blind to their own interests as to support another publican. They have an excellent candidate in Mr. Peter Peacock, who is making great headway in the contest. Rumour has it that still another publican contemplates contesting Bewsey Ward in the Tory interest. We hope he does—he is assured of a whacking I But the concentration of the Blues just now is in St. John's Ward, where Mr. Lash ford an estimable member of the butchering trade is seeking to oust a very useful and intelligent representative in Councillor Ball. This latest protegea of the Tory party is a man of few words, but much - docility. He held an open-air meeting on Monday and made a speech which was reported in the " Guardian " on Wednesday—verbatim we understand. It occu- pied four lines I Mr. Lashford said nothing which could be controverted, but any deficiencies in this respeat were kindly made up for him by other speakers. Councillor Steel's chief com- plaint against Councillor Ball was that he held forth on the claims of Socialism, and not oa the claims of St,John's Ward. A few minutes later Mr. Peter Rylands urged the electors to return Mr. Lashford in order to show that the Govern- ment of the country had outstayed its welcome. Evidently there are differences between Messrs. Steel and Rylands which had better be reconciled before they again make a joint appearance on a public platform. Mr. Steel's worship at the parish pump would seem to be more immediately service- able than Mr. Rylands' peculiar method of showing as he announced, "the interest he took in the municipal elections." That " interest " nearly resulted in the candidate for St. John's being submerged in a flood of words upon the surface of which imperial political topics floated aimlessly as corks.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Mr. Vistor Grayson, M.P. seems to find the times out of joint, but he won't remedy unemployment by making a row inside the House of Commons, any more than the suffragettes will obtain the vote for women by inciting riots outside the House. * * * In another column we publish the first of a series of " Questions for Tariff Reformers." We commend them to the study of our readers. They only need to try the experiment a time or two to discover how easily a well-planted question knocks the bottom out of the Tariff Reformer's specious arguments.

* Councillor Steel could not say much for the Tory candidate in St. John's Ward when he took the chair for him on Monday night, but he could and di'd say that the retiring member did not live in the Ward. Mr. Steel was forgetful of the simple fact that the other representatives of St. John's, himself and Coun- cillor Fulton-Smith, do not live in the Ward either.

• * * * Two delightful bulls were perpetrated at the Church Congress last week. At one meeting'—that to discuss the Position of the Laity—Canon A. W. Robinson told the meeting, in his opening remarks, that his words would be pointed to the verge of bluntness," and later in the evening, Sir A. Coote, explaining his presence at such a gathering, said that he was like "one of those satellites of Jupiter which, when they were Nisible, were always obscured." * * *

Councillor Ball evidentry knows something about electioneering. The day after Mr. F. T. Lashford's four-line speech to the electors of St. John's Ward had appeared in the press, his opponent challenged him to a public debate on municipal matters. This is real cute, as the Yankees say. Mr. Lashford might accept and stipulate that the debate should be limited to four lines on each side. This wouli put him on an equality with his challenger.* , The President of the Literary and Philosophical Society is sever so happy as when he can have a "go ' for Socialism. Last year he managed to find an opening in his Presidential address to denounce it, and on Monday evening he tried to show that Darwinigiii and Socialism are opposed to each other. It would be interesting to know under which heading —Literary or Philosophical Socialism comes and why Darwinism and Socialism are antagonistic. Perhaps Mr. A. J. Jolley will oblige next year. * * * The elections are already providing us with some gems in the way of political pronouncement. Mr. W. Peter Rylands is looked upon as the oracle of the Conservative party on Tariff Reform. Here is his creed in a nutshell ! Speaking at the open-air meet- ing in St. John's Ward on Monday Mr. Rylands said : "Not a single boot should come into this country as long as there was bootmaker out of employ- ment. (Applause.) Not a single article of men's clothing should come into this country as long as there was a tailor out of a job. (Applause.) A man shonld have the first call for the article he was producing. That was the main plank of the Conservative party." Poor plank ! But for " a man to have the first call for the article he is producing" is Socialism, is it not?

* * The Marquis of Ripon, despite his great age—he will be eighty-one on the 24th of this month—is not', the oldest living ex-Cabinet Minister. Viscount Cross is eighty-five, Viscount Knutsford and Earl Halsbury are both eighty-three, while Viscount Landaff is eighty-two. Lord Ripon, however, held Cabinet sank many years before either of them. It is an interesting fast that the venerable peer first entered the Cabinet as Secretary for War in 1803—a year in which three of his colleagues in the present Cabinet—Ml, Lloyd George, Mr. Harcourt, and Mr. Reginald McKenna— first saw the light. But a more interesting fact still is that he holds the record in the Liberal Party of the longest Parliamentary service —a period of fifty-six years—Lord Ripon having first sat in the House of Commons in 1852.

* * Mr. Winston Churchill received a handsome wedding present on Wednesday in recognition of his services to Manchester Liberalism. In his address of thanks he made an interesting allusion to the early days of his political career, and to his change of sides. The support of North-West Manchester had been, he said, the "turning point " in his political life : You trusted me, you took the spirit of my public womir, you ignored the words I had spoken, which I have regretted since,but whick were spoken in the days of youth and in the days when my political information was not what it has become. You allowed me to move in my own titne, as I chose and as I thought tit, and I think that the result was one you have no cause to regret. * * * The " other " Winston Churchill, the author of " Richard Carvell " "Mr. Crewe's Career," and other ibooks, is a native of St. Louis, U.S. The name by which he is known is a nom, do plume. He was educated at the Naval Academy at Annapolis, hence his success 0 a writer with naval scenes. Like many other millets, he began with journalism, being for a time editor of the "Army and Navy Journal" of New York-, and other publications. Some three years ago he married a rich St. :Louis Lady, and has been able since then to devote himself entirely to authorship. For some time after his marriage he lived in St. Louis, but later he bought a farm at Windsor, Vermont, on the upper reaches of the Connecticut River, and has there built ,a handsome residence. * * The House of Commons started work on Monday on the Children's Bill, and a good deal of progress was made under the vry capable guidance of Mr. Herbert Samuel. The Bill says that parents must be punished if they give alcohol to children under five, or go to bed drunk and " overlay " a child, or allow a child to fill into an unguarded fire. The first of these (the giving of drink to young children) raises certain difficulties, and was only carried by 123 to 103 on a division in which the question was left an "open" one ; but, speaking generally, the idea is that the elders must be restrained, so as to protect those who cannot protect themselves. It is not a pleasant thing to have to sit down and devise penalties for affairs of this kind; it would have been well if the policeman had not to be called in. But the practical point is that children are being injured by drink and cigarette-smoking, killed by over-laying, burnt by no-" guarded" fires. That being so we have to see if something cannot be done to put a stop to these blunders, which in this case assuredly amount to crimes, since they hurt the children.

* * * The question of how to deal with the " o verlaying " of infants is a painful one, and although it might have been pleaded that the limitation of space in very poor families should be considered, the statistics which Mr. Herbert Samuel gave, convinced the House that some such clause as the new one which ha intro- duced was necessary for the proper protection of infant life. Sixteen hundred cases of overlaying every year in England and Wales is a terrible record, and it is accentuated by the grim fact that the " accidents " are twice as numerous on Saturday nights as on any other nights in the week, and that when the week includes a Bank Holiday the death-rate goes up 50 per cent. Any lingering doubt as to the justice of dealing with the matter specially by legislation was dispelled when Mr. Samuel, who is both able and thorough in his grip of what he has in hand, cited the example of Germany, where by making overlaying a legal offence the deaths from this cause had been reduced almost to a minimum. There was reasonable hope of the Bill being got through before the debate on the Licensing Bill was entered upon, but thanks to the frivolous obstructionist tactics of Mr. Balfour and Sir Frederick Banbury, this object was not achieved. It is the business of the Opposition to oppose, of course, but they might at the same time show some regard for propriety and not trifle with the sacred cause of protecting child-life. The way Mr. Balfour talked " tommy-rot "trivialities about cigarette-smoking was positively indecent. We are tired of looking upon him as a serious politician with any settled convic- tions.

THE POLITICS OF THE WEEK.

[FROIS OUR LONDON CORRESPONDENT.] The work which lies before Parliament between now and Christmas is of exceptional importance. In . _

_ 'the Licensing Bill the Government have undertaken a task of whioh they fully realise the gravity and the difficulties. With the exception of the first clause, the Bill has yet to be carried through the Committee stage. The Miners' Eight Hours Bill, which also has to pass :through Committee, is another measure of great importance, although it does not arouse the sarael fierce hostility as the Licensing Bill. If, as there ley some reason to hope, the Education question could be settled by agreement before the end of the year, thie Autumn Sitting will indeed be a memorable one' Still, as Mr. Asquith said at Leeds on Saturday, even if the Session had been wound up at the beginning of. August, it would have been a memorable 'Session at the annals of Parliamentary history. The law which has been placed on the statute book to secure to ha a million of old men and women, the veterans of industry in this country, a sensible alleviation of thei ' wants and needs without any humiliating associatio and conditions stands out as a land-mark in the pro' gress of social reform.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
DISINTERESTED FOREIGN POLICY.

If fembers when they reassembled on Monday were conscious of the magnitude of the wohL: awaiting them, they also could not fail to be impressed by the seriousness of the circumstances under,which Parlia- ment re-opened. An imminent danger was brooding over the Eastern portion of Europe, and threatening to involve in its consequences, directly or indirectly, all the Great Powers. The gloom thrown by that disturbing knowledge was, however, lightened by an assured confidence in the justice and disinterestedness of the ground on which Great Britain had taken her stand. The whole nation is solidly united in support of the policy which is being pursued by his Majesty's Government. Lord Fitzmaurice in the House of Lords, and Mr. Asquith in the House of Commons, reaffirmed the principles on which that policy is based. They had already been clearly stated by the Pnime Minister and the Foreign Secretary in speeches outside the House : "It is irapossible for this country in the interest of the value of treaties to recognise an altera- tion of them made by any individual State without the consent of the other parties." The British Govern- ment has thehefore set itself to strive fcr a settlement 11 that will be both peaceful and equitable, with due regard to the interests of Turkey. Lord Lansdowne, as Leader of the Opposition' took occosicn to refer to the public statement made by Sir Edward Grey in the previous week—" Nothing," he said, "could have been more dignified in tone or more appropriate in sub- stance "—and to assure the Government of the co-operation of the Opposition in the accomplishment of their purpose.

UNEMPLOYED DEMONSTRATIONS. Unfortunately, the beginning of this last period of the Session has been signalised by some deplorable displays on the part of the advocates of woman suffrage and on the part of some of the unemployed and their leaders. Hunger and destitution are wild counsellors, and those who resort to violence under such an influence are to be pitied, however useless and harmful their action may be. But a terrible weight of respon- sibility rests upon the persons who appeal to the passions of starving men. It is not to be supposed that such demonstrations can produce any good effects. On the contrary, the only result is to deepen the pre- judice of those who are only too ready as it is to suspect and condemn any proposals that the Govern- ment may make for the relief of the poverty-stricken masses. The Prime Minister has promised that plans for the alleviation of urgent distress shall be completed with all possible dispatch, while a solution of the larger problem of dealing with the ultimate causes of uneuiployment will be steadily pursued. Reckless agitation only plays into the hands of reactionaries, who profess to detect a Socialist taint in any endeavour [ to ease the crushing pressure of poverty.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
TACTICS OF THE SUFFRAGISTS.

The disorder of the unemployed dernonstratizons ig not without the excuse of acute suffering It would be difficult to find any excuse for the unseemly con' duct which is working so much injury to the cause of woman franchise. The social problem is urgent, complicated, almost overwhelming. Parliament is struggling with an enormous burden of responsi- bilities, and every moment is precious. It is not reasonable, or fair, or creditable that the deliberations of Parliament should be interrupted by clamour for legislation which, as it would necessitate a funda- mental change in the electoral system, would post- ' pone every measure of social reform until a complex land bitterly controversial franchise question had been disposed of. The worst enemies of women's votes could not strike a more fatal blow at the cause than these agitators are doing, who attempt to sacrifice to their propaganda such vital issues as the remedy of unemployment, the promotion of temperance, and the protection of young children. OBSTRUCTING THE " CHILDREN"S CHARTER."

The "Children's Charter," whiela has been engaging the attention of tie House of Commons, is devoted to 'objects which are far too valuable to be made the I sport of party tactics. It was with real regret that many members of the Opposition discovered Lord Robert Cecil to be in alliance with Sir F. Banbury 'for the obstruction of the Bill's progress. As Mr. Samuel said on Monday, the Bill is better than a Government Bill; it is a House of Commons Bill, and membess on both sides had contributed to its use- fulness. The Government had shown a keen desire Cot to introduce controversial matters, and not to over- load the Bill. It was this latter consideration which induced Mr. Samuel to vote against Mr. Wedgwood 's amendment prohibiting the giving of alcohol to, children under the age of five, except upon the orders of a doctor—or nurse, as the new clause was subse- quently amended—or in case of sickness. The clause was a good one, but the Bill contain: so many valu- able provisions that it would be a great misfortune if its passage were , imperilled by grafting contentious additions upon it. FACETIOUS FACTIOUSNESS.

Mr. Balfour seemed to think that legislation against the evil of juvenile cigarette-smoking deserved no better treatment than to be made the peg to hang 3 number of indifferent jokes upon. But medical authorities and the results of experience in Army recruiting have pronounced very clearly on the deterioration in national physique which is produced by this habit. So to adapt legislation as to make it effective against the evil is admittedly diraoult. But when, as Mr. Samuel and Mr. Gulland pointed out,the tobacco trade have loyaNy accepted the provisions of the Bill, the hostile criticism of Mr. Balfour seems purely gratuitous.

AN EDUCATION SETTLEMENT. It would be foolish to minimise the obstacles that Mr. Runciman will have to overcome if a settlement of the Education controversy is to be arrived at by agreement. However, the Archbishop of Canterbury declared his belief, when opening some new Chore" schools at Croydon on Tnesday, that there is no need to " despair of a solution to the education problera which will preserve the principles for which each side cares most.' What those principles are, as far as the Liberal Party is concerned, Mr. Asquith once more proclaimed at Leeds. While he mentioned that he was not without hopes that the long-vexed question might at last be settled by something in the nature of a general agreement, he declared emphatically that the Government could never assent to any settlement which did not remove the grievance that money drawn from the pocket of the ratepayer was appropriated to the maintenance of sectarian teaching without popular control. THE ACCUSATION OF "VOTE-CATCHING," Because the Government has secured for the aged poor that boon of Old Age Pensions which the Con- sertatives had so long dangled before the constituents at election time, the Opposition accuses them of "Vote-catching." If that was the chief motive inspiring Liberal policy, it is astonishing, as Mr. Asquith has said, that the Government should have ventured upon their Licensing Bill. "It did not require verymuoh either of fancy or political insight to know that there would be a storm. When you gn tilting with all the forces of a Government and Parliamentary majority against the richest and best organized politically of all the great interests in this country, you are apt to be reminded that revolutions are not made with rose-water." The Government 110 shown itself ready to sacrifice popularity, and all the ordinary tactics of political warfare, for the sake of a great cause. It has a right, therefore, to call upon the Party for a great concentration of effort and for a triumphant display of union and discipline in order, to help to achieve the greatest and most fruitful el all social reforms."
