The Clerk read a circular letter from the Local Government Board with respect to the Old Age Pensions Act pointing out that it was provided under the regula- tions that for the purpose of enabling a pension officer to ascertain whether any person was disqualified by reason of the receipt of poor relief from receiving or continuing to receive a pension, every officer or person acting in the administration of the relief of the poor should, if so requested to do by. the pension officer supply to the pension officer such information as it was in his power to give. .TIIE:UNEMPLOYED QUESTION.

,1 The West Ham Union wrote pointing out the extreme urgency of the unemployed question and the great amount of distress prevalent amongst the working- class, and requesting the Board to urge the Prime Minister and the President of the Local Government Board to legislate in the present autumn session on the lines of providing useful productive worx for the unem- ployed workers. Mr. Mynekyme : One out of every twenty would work.

The Chairman: I am afraid there would a difficulty in finding useful work. Mr. Hallows: If we make work for the sake of mak- ing it, unless it is useful work we shall be doing more Berm than good. The Chairman: That is the dillieulty all public bodies have to face. It is all very well to ask them to find work.

Mr. Mynekyme said he went to Bridge Foot, Warrington, some weeks ago to Had some labourers. He told them it was a very easy job and the pay would be 4s. a day, but of twelve or fourteen men, not one would accept. Mr. Morris said he knew plenty of respectable men in Runcorn to-day who were walking about and would be only too glad to work for 4s. a day if they could get it. They were not all of the class Mr. Mynekyme mentioned. Mr. Mynekyme : I am speaking of Bridge Eoot, Warrington. Mr. Linaker : A special locality. Mr. Hallows : We have no right to spend people's money for the sake of making work. The Chairman: They want us to pester the poor Premier. I think he is pestered enough. It was decided that the letter should lie on the table.

NUMBER. OF _INMATES.

Mr. Spruce reported that the number of inmates of the Workhouse was 174 as compared with 117 for the corresponding period last year. STORES COMMITTEE.

It was proposed that Messrs. Hallows, Davies,Barter, Collins and Linaker jun., along with the Chairman and Vice-chairman of the House Committee should con- stitute a a Standing Committee to inspect the stores. A Member : What about Mr. Mynekyrae ? He has been inspecting stores lately. Mr. Morris : We are not expecting a wine cellar this time. (Laughter.) It was decided that Mr. Mynekyme's name be added to the committee.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. The fortnightly meeting of this Council was held at the Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. G. Pilling presided and there were also present :—Messrs. T. Wright, C. Roylance, J. Bolshaw, E. Britch, J. Stokes, A. Broome, T. Baxter, P. Booth, C. E. Linaker, J. H. Davies, L. Bolton, H. Mawdsley, S. Woodward, S. Horton, J. L. Jones, E. Bretherton, G. Wilson, J. NORLE7 VICAR AND THE kIIOOI7. WATER SUPPLY. The Clerk had received tLe following letter from the Rev. C. It. Nunn, Vicar of Norley, dated October 3th. 1903. Dear sir,—lZeturned home on Saturday last and have received notice to lay on water to the school in 14 days. Now apart from the fact that pure water of good quality is supplied fresh every day, contained in a closed and daily cleansed receptacle, and more in quantity than is ever required, it must be known that we are about to commence building'operations when the best arrangement of supplying the Liverpool water to the school as a complete whole will be considered and carried out. I therefore venture to ask if the Council will allow this matter to stand over a time in order that in view of the new room to be built and improve- ments made the plan of supply may be of the most convenient and adequate kind.—P.S. We are having a meeting within a week to consider the building of new rooms, &c. Mr. Woodward said that so far as he knew, until the previous evening, no managers meeting had been held at which the notice could have been considered by the managers, but it had been entirely dealt with by the corresponding manager without any reference to the will of the managers at all. He recommended that the communication be forwarded to the education adminis- trative sub-committee for the district. • Mr. Linaker : Was the subject of the letter dealt with by the managers last evening? Mr, Woodward : I cannot tell you.

The proposition was agreed to. THE REGULATION OF SLAUGHTER HOUSES. The Council resolved" That application be made to the Local Government Bcs.rd for an order under section 276 of the Public Health Act, 1873, investing the Rural District Council with the powers of the second and third paragraphs of section 169 and the whole of section 170 of the Public Health Act, 1373 ; also for an order investing the said Council with the powers of sections 29, 30 and 31 of the Public Health Acts Amendment Act 1390." _ _

It was intimated that the Council had made applica- tion to the Board for an order investing them with the powers of section 128 of the Towns Improvement Clauses Act 1840, which relates to slaughter houses, and the Board suggested the adoption of the above mentioned clauses, which incorporate section 123 and also enlarge the powers of the Council with respect to slaughter houses.

The West Ham Union wrote pointing out the extreme urgency of the unemployed question and the great amount of distress prevalent amongst the working class, and requesting the Board to urge the Prime Minister and the President of the Local Government Board to legislate in the present autumn session on the lines of providing useful productive work for the unem- ployed workers. The CLERK said this was a big question and they could not swallow it in a moment. The letter was referred to the House Committee. THE YEW GUARDIAN.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

A SOCIALIST CANDIDATE. The Earlestown Socialist Society have adopted Mr. Rowland King as their candidate to contest the War- grave Ward at the next Urban District Council election. FATAL FALL FROM. A SWINGBOAT. William Ellis, a tinsmith, employed at the London and North-Western Waggon Works, and residing at Lawrence-street, Earlestown, died on Tuesday at the Royal Infirmary, Liverpool, from injuries sustained through falling from a swingboat on the Market-place, Earlestown. . _

—The inquest was held at the Liverpool Coroner's Court on tVednesday.—lt appeared that the deceased, while the boat was in motion, fell off his seat on to the edge of another bort, awl was then thrown back. He sustained serious internal injuries, and he was operated upon at the Royal Infirmary, Liverpool, but died on Tuesday.—The jury found that death was acci- dental. MERIT CERTIFICA I'ES.

It will also serve as a standard of qualifi- cation which can be recognized and understood by parents, teachers, employers, and the public generally. MIDNIGHT ADVENTURE AT EARLESTOVi7N. _ _ _

At Earlestown, on Tuesday, Henry Billinge, Old-road, Latchford, was charged with breaking into the offices of the Vulcan Foundry, Limited, at Earlestown, and with wounding John Higgins, the night watchman. It is alleged that on September 25th last, Higgins was going his round of the works shortly after midnight, when he discovered the prisoner hiding in one of the offices, an entrance having been affected through a window. The prisoner who walked in his stocking feet, quietly submitted to capture, but whilst Higgins was sending for the police he savagely attacked him with a latge knife, stabbing him in the hand and arm, and inflicting live ugly wounds. Having overcome the watchman and taken his keys, prisoner made good his escape. All attempts to trace Billinge failed until Monday, when he was detained by the Rawtenstall police on a charge of theft. A remand to Warrington Ivas granted. Billinge was again brought up at the Warrington Occasional Court on Wednesday, before Mr. White.— John Higgins a night watchman of the Vulcan Foundry said he was going round the offices at 11.15 p.m. and went lathe general offices. He there found the prisoner in a crouching position underneath a desk. Prisoner was in his stocking feet. Witness asked him what he was doing there and he replied that he was having a rest, he told witness that he formerly worked at the Vulcan, and asked him to give him another chance. Witness asked him how he go in and he said he came in while a clerk was going out. Witness told defendant to put his clogs on, and he then took him to the lodge. Witness then told a work- man named Thomas Brockley to go and bring a police officer. After Brockley had gone witness locked the lodge door. Prisoner then asked if witness was going to let him go, and he replied that he had sent for a policeman. Prisoner then caught hold of witness and they struggled for three or four minutes. While struggling, the defendant pulled out a knife, resembling a shoemaker's knife, and attempted to stab witness in the face and chest, but witness warded off the blows with his hands. In consequence witness' hands were badly cut. Witness then became exhausted and the prisoner procured the keys which had fallen on the floor during the struggle, unlocked the door and escaped. Witness followed him but lost sight of him through the darkness. HS afterwards examined the premises in company with P.C. Lambert and found that a pane of glass immediately over a sash catch had been broken, and the catch pushed back. He also found that a drawer had been tampered with by cutting the woodwork around the lock with a knife. Witness next saw the prisoner at Rawtenstall Police Station and identified him from six other men. —Doctor Lathom of Earlestown, stated that at 1.30 it.na on Saturday, September 2tith,John Higgins was brought to his surgery by P.C. Lambert. He examined his hands and found he bade incised wound at the back of the fore-arm about 31 to 4in. in length extending in depth down to the bone. Higgins had also an incised wound at the back of the right thumb about 11in. in length, also a wound on the right wrist joint about 3in. in length, and an incised wound between the left thumb %and fore-finger about 4in. in length. The wounds were of recent origin, and had been caused by a sharp instru- ment. Higgins was suffering from shock. Some of the veins were divided. Prisoner had nothing to say to the charges and was sent for trial at the Preston Quarter Sessions.

ST. HELENS.

LIFE IN ST. HELENS.

On Monday, Robert Shepherd (1.11), of Napier-street, was charged with assaulting a little girl named Agnes Tinsley, aged under four years. The alleged offence took place on Friday last. Shepherd lives next door to the little girl, and he decoyed her into the backyard, where they were discovered by the girls mother. Shepherd's mother cvas stated by the Chief Constable to be in the workhouse infirmary owing to her drunken habits ; and the youth lived in most horrid surround- ings."—The youth was put under the probation officer for three years, and the father was bound over in £5 to improve the condition of`the home.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
DID OT UNDERSTAND IRE RULES.

_ " John Smith " was given, at St. Helens on Monday, as the name of an Austrian Pole who was charged with lodging in the open air. " Pole, " speaks broken English, and has ideas of self-help which are founded on back to the land "principles, for Police-constable ' Charlton said that early on Sunday morning he saw a fire in one of the plantations near Sherdley Hall. He went into the park, and found this man sitting behind a tree in front of the fire. " Smith " said he had come Irons Manchester, and he was cold, and so he lit a fire. He did not understand the rules •, but he had worked in a colliery at St. Helens. He had only been in England a year.— Smith " was sent to Walton for fourteen days. ALLEGED WORKHOUSE BUNGLE.

George Taylor,'a St. Helens labourer, was up before the magistrates on Monday on a warrant charg- ing him with being £22 10s. in debt to the Prescot Board of Guardians for the maintenance of his daughter. Taylor left the child in January, 1900, when she was eight years old. In November of the same year he was brought before the magistrates and given time in which to take the child out.—Taylor said that he went on seven Saturdays for his child, and the work- house people could not find her. They did not know whether she was at the workhouse in Fazakerley Homes or at Pautasaph. They promised to let him know when they found her, but he had not been notified. He pro- duced a letter dated January last year, which bore out his statement.—Mr. Richardson, relieving officer, asked for a remand so that the whole case could be properly investigated.—The magistrates agreed.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

St. Luke's Church.

The harvest festival services at the above church were commenced on Thursday evening when there was a large congregation. The church was handsomely decorated with flowers, fruit, and cereals and much credit is due to the ladies who undertook the work. The Rev. G. C. Briggs Vicar of Christ Church, Latch- ford, occupied the pulpit, and there was special music by the choir. Wycliffe Cockhedge Mission.

Harvest thanksgiving service; were held at this mission, Brick-street, on Sunday, when the preacher at all the services was the Rev. J. S. Langley,of Kirkham. The sacred edifice ladd been prettily decorated with corn, flowers and fruit presented a pleasing appearance. There were good congregations, and the discourses of the rev, gentleman were listened to with great interest. The special music was a very attractive feature, and reflected credit upon the choir.

Legh-Street Primitive Methodist Church. The Christian Endeavour anniversary was celebrated at this church on Sunday, when Mr. J. \V. Jenkinson, of Hartley College, preached. In the evening the choir rendered the anthem "The radiant morn," and Miss A. Bridge gave a solo entitled "The glorious city." In the afternoon the Junior Christian Endeavourers held their their anniversary when they contributed an exercise led by Miss Baglass. Mr. Jenkinson gave an excellent address and Mr. H. J. Billington presided, assisted by Mr. J. Landor. The services were concluded on Mon- day evening when the society received a visit from the President of the Union (Rev. J. L. Milne). Mr. A. Spruce was the conductor and Mr. B. Clatworthy, the Organist.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. CAMPBELL MORGAN. -- VISIT TO WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

Warrington was yesterday honoured with a visit from the Rev. G. Campbell Morgan, D.D., the eminent preacher, of London, who occupied the pulpit at Wycliffe Congregational Church in the afternoon and evening. Tall and somewhat ascetic in appearance, the rev, gentleman combines scholarly Biblical exposition with evangelical fervour, and his discourses were listened to with keen interest by large congregations *which included representatives of many denominations. In the afternoon the preacher based his sermon on the part that mountains played in the lifo of Christ. It was on the mountain that Jesus proclaimed what some people termed the Sermon on the Mount but what he preferred to call the Manifesto of the King. Never perhaps had that sermon obtained so much attention as it did at the present time. Men were pointing to that sermon as containing the ethic which ought to guide and dominate all civic, national and racial life. They were perfectly right, said the preacher, i but unfortun- ately they forgot that the only way n which that ethic could be applied to national and civic life was to apply it to individual life. The ethic of the Sermon on the Mount was unattainable apart from the dynamic of the Cross. The Mount of Transfiguration marked the culmination of the humanity of Christ. There Christ became what God originally intended man to be. The other mountain to which the preacher referred was the mount in Galilee on which Christ took farewell of His disciples and sent them forth to disciple the Inations. The respective mountains were termed the Mount of Light, the Mount of Life, and the Mount of Love. The preacher concluded by pointing out that whilst Christ loved the mountains, He lived in the valleys. On the mount:fin He gained His inspiration ; in the valley He did His work. The Church was in danger of forgetting this. She had been too long on the mountain heights and rejoicing in her own light, had wasted spiritual force upon herself. The cry of the hour was : Back to the valleys, to the places where humanity in its groaning and agony was crying out for God and could not find Him. It could only find him as He was incarnate through us in the fulfilment of the life of Christ. No man, declared the preacher, could begin to minimise the meaning of sin and its awfallness without suffering moral degeneration in his own life. They might have a philosophic system that pleased the mental mood of the hour, but no evangel. They might have a social dream that seemed as though it ought to weak, but no evangel. There was no evangel for poisoned, paralysed humanity but the evangel of blood and resurrection. In the evening Dr. Campbell Morgan took for his text the words" All things are delivered unto Me of My Father; neither knoweth any man the Father, save the Son, and he to whomsoever the Son will reveal Him. Come unto Me all ye that labour, and I will give you rest. Take My yoke upon you and learn of Me ; for lam meek and lowly in heart ; and ye shall find rest unto your souls. For Mv yoke is easy. and My burden is light." (Matthew XL 27 to 30). The fueda- mental and underlying teaching of the passage, said the preacher, was that all restlessness was the outcome of lack of knowledge of God. Jesus stood in the midst of a restless, feverish age, whether they thought of that in which He actually uttered the words or of this age in which He still uttered them. He called men to Himself that they might find rest. But what was the rest that He offered them? That knowledge of God which, being possessed, con- strained men to submission to God, in which submission rest was to be felt. No words that ever fell from the lips of Jesus had made a profounder impression on the heart of humanity than these. All ages, all climes, all conditions, all castes were attracted by them. Why? 1 The answer that came most readily was "The simplicity of them" and yet that was not altogether a sufficiest answer. There was a simplicity which was the simplicity of superficiality. These words had retained their perennial freshness through nineteen cen- turies, and while to-day they were translated into 400 different languages, wherever they went they still made their appeal. If tile simplicity were that of superficiality, the charm word soon wear away. There was, however, a simplicity of sublimity and that was what he found in these words. "For myself " continued the preacher" I would lay ray hands on this passage and call it the snpreme and final proof-text of the absolute deity of the Lord Jesus. It contains not in detailed exposition but in germ and principle the whole law and gospel of the great God I and ef our Saviour Jesus Christ." Thomason why this call of Jesus had made such an appeal to humanity and still does, was that here deep calleth unto deep. It was an appeal to leave that which was false and find the way into the true, to depart from the region of ruin and to enter into the realms of realisation by redemption. In every life there was a master passion and that constituted the burden. In other words the burden and responsibility of life might be noble or ignoble, beautiful or base, magnificent or mean. The master passion of the life of Jesus was to do the will of God and in the text Jesus declared that the easiest thing in the world was to please God. "I would rather have to please C-od for a year," said the preacher, " thaa any of you for a week. I am quite sure you would be as reasonable as you could, but von wonld not be as reasonable as God or as patient." ).esus Christ divided humanity into two camps—men whose burden was the will of God and men whose burden was their own desire, the self-centred and the God-centred life, and there was no middle path. "Come unto Me," said Christ, not "Come to the Church." No man got to Christ through the Church ; a man might get to the Church through Christ. " Come unto Me " —there was no room for any middle man there. "Your soul to Christ," said the preacher. "Everyone else swept out of the way. Priest and preacher too, at one point must depart.'

Illness of Mrs. W. Pierpoint. Our readers will regret to hear that Mrs. Pierpoint, wife of Alderman Pierpoint, J.P., is lying seriously ill at her residence, Derbe-road, St. Annes-on-Sea.

foreign firms who had works in England. The Govern- ment's aim was to place taxation on the shoulders of those best able to bear it. The Government believed ip the great reform of the land question : "To every man a piece of land, and to every piece of land a man.' It had been pointed out that the Government made no provision in the Budget for the grant they promised to Ireland. The Opposition could not have their eyes open to these great matters, or they would have seen that since the Government gave Ireland Home Rule the people of that country had become so prosperous that they did not need the money. (Laughter and applause) The grant of £1,000,000 to the unemployed was said to be a paltry sum,but it was a grand and noble beginning. The question would not be settled by one Bill. It eould need a long succession of measures and systematic work. The Socialists did a great deal in pointing out the evils that existed, but they seemed to have no, practical policy to remove them. He was afraid that if the Government remained in office long enough tile occupation ot the Socialists would be gone, because by progressive legislation the Government would have I removed all the evils they complained of. (Applause). Mr. IL IloPs (Cambridge) said that enterprise, capital and labour should go hand in hand, and it the Govern- ment set one against the other and over-taxed the wealthy, they would do great harm to trade. Why did not the Government tax • wines and spirits? They might next take up the Co-operative Societies with their £300,000,000 capital—(Opposition applause and a Voice " You have got your eyes on those, have you ? ")—and also the insurance societies, which on paper had practically the whole wealth of the world. (Laughter.) As to unemployment, the casual workers and the preponderance of unskilled labour were caused by ignorance and selfishness on the part of parents, and by the mistaken policy of trade unions in keeping down the number of apprentices. Mr. J. MORTON (President ef, the Board of Trade) said the Government wanted so to alter the industrial system that the question of unemployment would be got rid of on economic lines. They did not want artificial employment and they must devote their attention to finding a solution by the modification of the industrial system. " The more progressive the Liberal party is " said Mr. Morton " the more our friends opposite say we are pandering to the Socialists, whereas the Socialists themselves will admit that they I are simply a more extreme spction of our own party." (Socialist : " No, No's.") Mr. WARDHAN (Westlionghton) moved an amend- ment "That it be illegal to levy any soloing royalties except for State purposes." Ho did so on the ground of equity, fgirness and reason. Mr. W. NIGHTINGALE (Normanton) seconded and caused great amusement by observing " e do not want simply the royalties. What we want ano what w,e mean to have is the earth." "We are the nation " he continued "and we shall have power in the future that will compel every class to subordinate themselves to us."

Ultimately the amendment was withdrawn in order to facilitate procedure, but Jr. Nightingale dissented frog: this course. Mr. W. CFIRIAIES (S. Birmingham), said he was much in sympathy with the object of the Budget but they must give Mr. Chamberlain credit for having first pro- posed these reductigps. (A Voice : Like he did old age pensions.") ThEfthancellor of the Exchequer was I only appropriating a portion of the tariff reform pro- 'gramme. (Laughter.) Mr. C'hrimes admitted that he was not very hostile to the Budget and that he did not see anything very unjust in the manner in which the Chancellor proposed to raise the money,assuming it was practicable. On the whole the imposition of higher death duties was just, but if they went very far with regard to increased income tax, they would certainly drive capital out of the country. THE CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER (Mr. England), replying to the criticism about robbing hen roosts, said the people of the country were really laying the golden eggs, but how many did they get hold of ? The wealthy had in years gone by taken the golden eggs and in many cases the Government would be doing right in restoring the whole of them to the people, but seeing they did not require all the wealth of the country they only proposed to take a small tithe.

The Budget was then put to the vote and passed without a di,vision.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

A Discussion on Tooth Brushes.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse, on Wednesday, the Rev. T. R. Matthews presiding. There were also present : Mrs Simpson, Mrs. Wilkinson, Mrs. Collingwood, Miss Broadbent, Dr. Murray, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, W. Gill, W. C. Brooks, J. Billinge, R. Lee, W. Furness, M. W. Seton, W. Flannagan, J. Cook, R. Bolton, E. L. Bent, J. Evans, R. Forshaw, A. Bottomley (clerk), R. W. Peacock (assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintqlgapt of Padgate Homes.) PADGATH St INTENDENT'S SALARY.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
A DISCUSSION ON TOOTH BRUSHES.

At a meeting of the Padgate Cottage Homes Com- mittee it was recommended that the salary of Mr. Wright (superintendent) be increased from £35 to £6O per annum, also that the Wary of Miss Alice Bennett be increased from £2O to £23 by annual increments of £l. It was recommended that Mr. Taylor, Museum-street, Warrington be given the dentistry work for the ensuing six months, subject to his terms being satisfactory. It was moved by Miss Broadbent, seconded by Mr. Bent that the children be provided with tooth brushes, but the proposal was defeated by 7 votes to 4. Mrs. COLLING WOOD : It seems rather extraordinary not to allow the children tooth brushes.

Miss BROADBENT : I don't think there is any other institution of the same size where they do nct have them. _ . .

The CHAIRMAN : It is the decision of the committee I don't know whether it is irrevocable or not. Mr. BENT in moving the adoption of the minutes said he was very sorry that the children were not to be pro- vided with tooth brushes. The cost would be very little—a matter of about 13—and he was satisfied that the provision of tooth brushes would save a great deal of expense in dental operations. The increase of salary to Mr. Wright was considered by the committee and carried unanimously, as they were all agreed that he had done his work in the best possible way. Mrs. WiLiumoN seconded. Mr. FURNESS said there was at least one dissentient from the resolution increasing Mr. Wright's salary. He dissented from it not because he took exception to Mr. Wright's abilities as an administrator—he was an ideal administrator and the establishment was in a perfect condition—but because the granting of the increase had been rushed. It was done almost in a panic. He suggested that the Board ought to be furnished with particulars as to the amount paid in other Unions and satisfy themselves that they were doing justice not only to the superintendent but to the ratepayers as a whble. He moved that the portion of the minutes referring to Mr. Wright's salary be sent back to the Committee for further consideration. _ _

- -- Mr. EVANS seconded but on a vote being taken the amendment was defeated by ten votes to seven. Mr. BENT gave notice that at the next Board meeting he would move a special resolution confirming the increases of salary to Mr. Wright and Miss Bennett as recommended by the Committee. Mr. JOLLEY then m6ved an amendment that the minute with regard to tooth brushes he referred back. He contended that the matter was not a small one and the proposal ought to have been adopted long since. Why the children, because they happened to be in a Poor Law school, should be deprived of a rational and proper means of keeping their mouths clean and in order, he could not understand. Mr. EVANS seconded. Miss BROADBEN'T said the children already tried to clean their teeth with bits of rag but it was impossible to keep them in proper condition by that means. Mrs. Wilkinson thought there would be a certain amount of risk of carrying consumption but she had it on good authority that the risk was so small that it need not be considered, whereas the advantages were enormous. There was a very strong feeling in the town on the matter,

The CHAIRMAN: Would it not be a better plan to supply them with tooth brushes privately, if the resolu- tion is lost ?

Miss BROADBENT : Mr. Furness suggested they should be supplied by the Brabazon Society, but I don't see why the Guardians should palm off their responsibili- ties on to that organization. Mr. FLANNAGAN said the Committee thought the provision of tooth brushes would possibly result in more harm than good. The brushes might be exchanged and become the means of conveying disease. If he could be satisfied that each child had a separate brush which could not be exchanged he would support the proposal. The CLERK : The Superintendent said he would see that the children kept their own brushes. The amendment was carried by 13 votes to 5.. Miss BROADDENT : I think that is one of the best things we have ever done. ____ _

PENSIONS AND OUT-RELIEF.

MRS. WILKINSON'S PROPOSITION OUT or ORDER..

Mrs. WILKINSON had given notice of the following resolution: 'That all persons receiving relief from this Union, and having no other disqualification for Old Age Pension, shall, after the first day of January 1909, receive five shillings per week for each single person, and eight shillings per week in the case of married couples." The CfiAirtmAls : I am advised by the Clerk that the resolution is not quite in order. Mrs. WILKINSON : Why not in order, I should like to ask.

man• The CLERK said the reason why the resolution was not in order was because the Guardians could not bind themselves to a fixed amount when granting out-door relief. The question which the Guardians had to con- sider was destitution and they had to deal with each case on its merits. The resolution would commit the Guardians to a fixed sum and there would be no consideration whatever. Under those circumstances it would be offensive. The opinion of the Local Govern- ment Board had been asked and that authority did not consider it would be a proper exercise of the power of the Guardians to order a fixed weekly sum as out-relief to all persons above a certain age. The amount of relief to be given should be determined by the necessities of each case. Mr. WILKINSON : Some Boards have done it. Thc CLERK : I agree that some Boards have done it; many Boards have done wrong. I should say that it will be a question for the Government auditor to deal with. We can do many things that are opposed to orders but we have to run the risk. Clearly this is a letter from the Local Government Board who tell you it is not the proper thing for Guardians to do, and under those circumstances I think I must be right in saying the resolution is not in order. Mrs. WILKINSON: I think it is most unjust for any- bOdy to be deprived of any advantages owing to having been poor prior to a certain date. If we cannot alter such cruel laws we can help those who are made to suffer by them through no fault of their own. The CLERK : If the Guardians wish to consider the matter at some future date—because it seems rather hard I should have to say it is out of order—l have drafted a resolution which they might discuss. Mr. JOLLEY : I think we ought to discuss this resolution or nothing. Let notice be given if any fresh matter is to be brought forward. Mr. W. C. BROOKS : What would be the increased amount on the rates if this were adopted ? The CLERK submitted the following statement :

LOYAL ORDER OF ANCIENT SHEPHERDS (AU.) WARRINGTON DISTRICT.

I This district held its quarterly meeting at the " Friend in Need " Lodge, " ‘Vaggon and Horses," Warrington, on Saturday. The District Chief (Bro. J. Gillooley) presided and was supported by the Deputy Chief (Bro. W. Miles), Past Prov. Chiefs Peter L. Edgar, Lawrence Weir, T. Miller, W. Eckersley, J. Haley, J. I Williams, James Parr (District Treasurer), Peter Edgar (District Secretary) and the delegates from the Lodges belonging to the District. The quarterly accounts were submitted by the District Secretary, and after the Auditor's (Bros. Miller and Miles) having certified their correctness, the same were passed after the remaining business, which was of a routine character, had to be disposed of, an interesting and pleasing ceremony was performed, viz.,the presentation to the Immediate Past Chief Bro. L. Edgar, of a handsome walnut clock for valuable services rendered by him during his term of office as Pros-. Chief Shepherd of the district. Bro. Lawrence Weir in a few well chosen words made the presentation. The following Brethren, viz., John Haley, Thomas Miller, S. Blythe, James Parr, Jas. Qillooley, and W. Miles spoke in support. Bro. "Peter L. Edgar on rising to acknowledge the gift was received with enthusiasm. He said no words of his could express his heartfelt thanks for their Lind and flattering remarks and the handsome present. What he had done had been work of pleasure, his chief desire being to see the Shepherds Order prosper and flourish, and it was gratifying to note that his efforts had been appreciated. His term of office had been a happy time! He desired to thank the members generally of the different Lodges for the great courtesy they ha.d at. all times shown towards him on his round of visitation. The meeting confirmed the officers' action in regard to Use resolution that they had passed and sent in to the Town Clerk in connection with the appointment of the I Local Committee under the Old Age Pensions Act.

Patrons of the Royal Court Theatre are promises another treat next week when Mr. Herbert Greville's company, by arrangement with Mr. Charles Frohman, will present "Merely Mary Ann," a highly successful 'comedy in four acts by I. Zanrovill. The company comes from the Duke of York's Theatre, London, and is an excellent one, inclnding several artistes of con- siderable repute. Miss Jessica Solomon will appear as " Mary Ann, merely," Mr. Paul Lovett as Lancelot, a composer, Mr. Herbert Greville as Lord Tottenham, of the Automobile Club, and Miss Grace Lovett as Rowena. The play is sure to provide a capital evening's amusement and there .should be crowded houses.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

THE BUDGET PASSED.

Private Member's Bill Next Week.

The Chancellor of the Exchequer (Mr. E. England) has succeeded in carrying his first Budget through our local House of Commons. For once in a way, the Opposition did not even challenge a division, but it was evident from the strong chorus of "Ayes" that an overwhelming majority of the members favoured the Chancellor's progressive proposals. Briefly stated, the Budget provides for the abolition of all food taxes, the granting of old age pensions, and an instalment of £1,000,000 for the unemployed. The necessary funds are to be raised by increased death duties, graduated income tax, and the payment of a fourth of the mining royalties into the National Exchequer. There was a good attendance of ministers,headed by the Premier (Mr. John Upson), but the Leader of the Opposition (Mr. W. IL Stansfleld) was an absentee. QUESTION TIME.

Replying to the member for Maidstone, the CRANCELLOR OF THE EXCIIEQUER (Mr. E. England) said the amount of mining royalties paid to the land owners of the country was about £8,000,000 per annum and it was continually increasing. The average royalty was 3.d. per ton. . .

ThesMimi3Ert FOR WEST DERRY asked if the Govern- ment would consider the advisability of amending the Old Age Pension Act with a view to making it obliga- tory on pension authorities to co-opt representatives from friendly societies and trade unions upon their committees.

The PnEmma (Mr. John Upson) said the Govern- ment had a great deal of sympathy with the question. At the same time it could hardly be expected, seeing that the Pension Act did not come into force until January Ist, 1909, that they shoukl amend it until by experience they had proved its weakness or its strength It was, however, generally anticirtted that pension authorities,in other words the Town Councils,would avail themselves of that expert opinion referred to in the qvestion. The Government were only aware of one Council containing members of such great knowledge and wisdom and so many experts upon every subject under the sun, that they could without any ditticulty at all-and without calling in the information of those men who had been interested in these questions for many years, adequately and fully carry out the Act. (Laughter and applause). The MENtnErt FOR NORTEIWICII asked the Chancellor of the Exchequer if he would consider the suggestion of imposing a tax on foreign made toys and so increase the revenue of the country and stimulate British industries.

The 61-lA.NCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER (Ir. E. England) : It is not our intention to impose any such tax, because we do not for a moment believe there is any necessity to impose taxes upon any industries, foreignor home. There are so many ways by which revenue could be obtained.

Mr. W. CIIIIIMES (S. Birmingham) : Honestly? The liirAscELLOR Yes, honestly. - In reply to the Member for East Belfast, theTonErcs SECRETARY (Mr. E. Wynne-Jones) assured the House that all necessary steps were being taken to protect British interests in the Near East. DEATE ON THE BUDGET.

Mr. W. FURNESS (Bristol) re-opened the debate on the Budget. Lie did not tbink for a moment that they were going to bring the millennium by taxation or that by that means they would elevate the social condi- tions of the country. The Opposition said the only panacea was tariff reform, but England would have none of that. (Applause.) The Government bad found a better method of raising the funds than tariff reform. Dealing with the bogey of invasion which seemed to Itrouble the other side, Mr. Furness said the Liberal Government, as a progressive rind economical Govern- ment, had made up their minds that so far as the naval armaments of this country were concerned, they were not going to make any reduction or take any risks. At the present time we had 37 battleships, while Germany had 11 and France 11. We had 32 armoured cruisers, Germany G and France 13. %Ye were building at the rate of something like three per annum, and if the process continued in the same ratio we should in 1911 have 103 war vessels, as com- pared with Germany 51 and France 41. Dealing with mining royalties, Mr. Furness said he failed to see how the diversion of all mining royalties from private ownership to the State would enable British manufac- turprs to compete on better terms. What was required was the abolition of the royalties, and to do that they must either purchase the royalties from the owners or achieve their end by annual curtailment of the royal- ties, so that the owners would not feel the sacrifice so keenly. Whatever taxes the Govern- ment had abolished to lighten the burdens of the middle and poorer classes, had called forth complaint from the Leader of the Opposition. The Government were not surprised at that because it was a matter of common history that Conservative Governments bad always been extremely loath to take off taxes (Jr. ChrimeS "Nonsense:) Mr. Furness had a word to say about the "avalanche of ideas" which was always fosthcoming from the fertile brain and imagination of of the member for Northwich. "We can only regret" he observed that "our mental capacity on this side of the House has not been quite equal to the occasion." (Laughter.) The chief cause of national decay, con- cluded the hon, member, was excessive taxation and therefore the Government were doing a goon work in abolishing so many taxes. (Applause.) Mr. F. De:cez (Portsmouth), in a maiden speech from the Independent benches, said the Government had disclaimed any intention of interfering with industries, but had they already forgotten the Licensing Bill and the hundreds of thousands of sober-minded citizens who went up to London to protest against it? (Laughter.) As to mining royalties, why did the Chancellor purpose to appropriate only a fourth of them ? Was it not a breach of trust to allow the remaining V,000,000 to go into the pockets of people who had no right to them because they really belonged to the State ? He was sorry the taxation of land values had been shelved, because it had been repeatedly promised by the Govern- ment. If they dealt with the matter by a Bill it would no doubt be sent back by the Lords. The idea of " fill- ing the cup "might be very good, but it was not states- manship, and the Government should have included !their proposals in the Budget. (Applause.) Mr. McDmtatro (Paisley) waxed humorous about the " rob:Aug of hen roosts" by the Government. "I have been told " he said" that the demand for barbed wire and other accessories for the protection of these humble abodes of roosters has been excessive in this district, and in some parts, especially near the place where the Chancellor resides, they have assumed alarm- ing proportions, something after the style of the block houses in the late war." (Laughter.) Mr. McDermid said he welcomed the proposals for the abolition of taxes on the articles of food enumerated in the Budget —in fact he claimed that it was part of his party's scheme—but where they differed was as to the means of raising the revenue. When the Chancellor came to that question, he discarded the hen roost robber's costume and assumed the character of the valiant highwayman. He set out to rob the living and plunder the dead. (Laughter.) Mr. McDermid emphasised the necessity for dealing with unemployment at its source and claimed that his party had put forward a practicable scheme which was more constitutional than the methods pro- posed in the Budget. Mr. E. WYNNE JONES (Foreign Secretary) said that after last week's admission from the Opposition things could hardly be moving pleasantly in that quarter. The Leader of the Oppositioh said he would tax beer up to the very hilt, and they hoped he had not had a very bad time on that account. (Laughter.) Ile also said he would tax everything that was injurious. That was the reason why the Chancellor of t'le Exchequer was going to tax the very rich. They were injurious; at any rate, they were not conducive to the welfare of the country. PRESENTATION TO EX-SUPERINTENDENT MONAGHAN.

An interesting ceremony took place on Monday morning when ex-Superintendent Monaghan, late of the Borough Police Force, was presented by the borough magistrates with a handsome oak smoking cabinet in recognition of his 28 years' services. The magistrates present were the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst), Alderman Bolton (deputy-Mayor), Alder- man Burton, and Messrs. S. Wiight, G. Pilling, W. Roberta, T. Locker, J. Rymer Young and E. Woods, with Mr. George F. B. White (magistrates clerk), Mr. William Parsons (assistant clerk), and Mr. Martin Nicholls (chief constable.) Mr. W. ROBERTS, before asking the Mayor to make the presentation, said there had not been a single refusal in raising the money, and it seemed to have been the pleasure of all to be associated with the presentation. One of the greatest tributes that could be paid to the impartiality of their late Superintendent was a little incident which occurred in the Court during the last week of Mr. Monaghan's stay with them. An old Irishman was being charged with having been drunk and disorderly and pleaded not guilty. The Superintendent went into the box and gave evidence to the effect that he saw the prisoner when he was brought in rt nd that there was no doubt he Was very drunk. The old Irishman then at once said, " Your Worships, if Mr. Monaghan says I was drunk, I must have been drunk." (Laughter). Mr, Monaghan had not only enjoyed the confidence of the magistrates, but their good-will, as well as the con- fidence of the townspeople generally, and even that of the prisoners who, unfortunately, had been brought before the Court from time to time. (Hear, hear.) The MAYOR said he considered it an honour as well as a pleasnre to accede to the request to make the presentation on behalf of his fellow magistrates. As Mr. Roborts had stated, it had been a great pleasure to all of them to subscribe to the testimonial. The Superintendent had been complimented, not only at the Assizes but in other places, and there was no doubt he had done good work on behalf of the borough. It would be interesting to the magistrates to know that Mr. Monaghan retired from the Warrington Police a few weeks ago (August 27th), after a service of over 28 years. He joined the force in 1880, when aged 21, as an ordinary constable. It was evident from his subsequent career that he at once applied himself diligently to his duties, and, possessed as he was of great natural shrewd- ness, besides being gifted with the true detective instinct, he was early marked for promotion. After four years' service he was awarded the merit badge. In 1892 he was promoted detective-sergeant, and in 1897 detective-inspector, while a year later saw him superintendent and second in command of the force. During the whole of his police career ' Mr. Monaghan was much in touch with the magistrates, first as an officer, making numerous and frequent arrests, and afterwards when in charge of prosecutions, whilst acting as the Chief Constable's deputy. From his long connection with the town and the force he was always I able to give justices useful and valuable information respecting prisoners and defendants coming before the Bench. (Hear, hear.) His worship then handed to the ' ex-Superintendent a handsome and massive polished oak smoker's cabinet. containing every requisite. On a brass plate on the front was the inscription :—" Pre- sented to Superintendent P. Monaghan by the magistrates of the borough upon his retirement from the police force, Warrington, October 12, 1908." Mr. MONAGIIAN, in reply, said it had always been his wish to assist the magistrates in giving them the infor- mation they required. If a man deserved punishment, he always told the Bench that he deserved it, but if, on the other hand, he could say anything in his favour, he said it. He had been very well treated in Warrington, and he should be sorry to lose his friends wheu the time came for him to go.

TO SAVE THE CHILDREN.

A !Temperance Rally In Manchester.

LICENSING BILL SUPPORT.

IThe Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope and Temperance Union held two remarkable meetings in the Free-trade Hall on Saturday. The gathering in the afternoon was described as a"Counties Workereßally," and its object TM to inaugurate a forward movement for doubling the membership of the Bands of Hope and juvenile temperance societies in the Union and to raise a sum of £12,500, equivalent to a shilling a head on the present membership. Ninety-one towns and 117 villages in Lancashire. Cheshire, and North-west Derbyshire were represented by over three thousand accredited delegates from 1,233 Bands of Elope, Itechabite "Tents," and Divisions of the Sons of Temperance. Canon Hicks,the president of the Union. presided, and those present included Mr. D. J. Shackleton, M.P. (vice-president), Mr. J. S. Higham, M.P. (treasurer), the Rev. Canon Kelly, the Rev. J. Cregan, Hr. T. E. Hallsworth (hon. secretary), and Mr. W. Chandos Wilson, the general secretary. A special feature of the meetings was the flue singing of a choir of COO voices selected from temperance societies in various towns included in the Union.

A CAUSE FOR THE CHURCHES. Canon Ifrcrig said this movement belonged to the Church, for the Church would never be in a flourishing state as long as the liquor traffic prevailed as it did now. The present year would always remain a very important year in the annals of temperance reform, because we had had the strongest and most comprehen- sive bill brought in by one of the strongest and most democratic Governments and Parliaments we had ever had. --(Applause.) [he Liberal party were solid for the Bill, and he thought he might say the Labour party were white-hot for it. If the Bill got through the House of Lords—that august assembly which seemed to direct the fortunes of the British people—they would want an increasing number or intelligent and convinced temperance reformers, wir,, when the Act got into operation, would see that no disaster over- took it and that the Lords blind to their own danger, did not imperil one of the greatest pieces of moral legislation ever attempted in England. (Applause.) In the meantire there was a great deal of spadework to be done in the creation of public opinion in favour of the measure. Mr.ch had been said about conciliating the moderate opinion of the country. By all means ; but they were nnderred by any danger, unmoved by any allurments, in their determination to achieve the prin- ciple of the originators of the movement—total abstinence for the individual. Their business was to save the children, for in saving the childen they were saving themselves. (Applause.) Mr. J. S. Higham, M.P., spoke of the progress made by the Union during the forty-five years of its exist- ence. It began, he said, with twelve societies and 1.000 members. It now numbered 2,1.00 societies and 385,000 members. The income it received was utterly inadequate if they were to deal with the work which lay in their hand, and helooked forward to a determined and successful effort to achieve the end which their present forward movement had in view. LABOUR PARTY AND THE BILL. MR. SIIACKLETON'S SUPPORT.

Mr. D. J. Shackleton, M.P., moved the following resolution "That we welcome the great forward movement proposals, and hereby pledge ourselves individually and collectively to do our utmost to double the membership and to raise a sum equal to one shilling per head of the present membership." He said that apart altogether from temperance, all social questions depended upon a sober nation, and if the little party to which he belonged was to be successful they mnst depend, on a sober nation. " That," Mr. Shackleton said, "is why you find the Labour party unanimous in support of the present Licensing Bill. They have not supported that Bill because it was introduced by the Liberal Government. They have sannortei it because they see in it great possibilities. When the nation has had this Bill—as they will—for some years they may desire some further legislation. The result of the Ligensing Act—when it is an Act—will be to improve the stamina of the people. Our Bands of Hope will do the same thin, and the two forces together will make a wonderful change in the condition of our people during the neat fifteen years." He believed as a workman they had a good work in hand, land he asked that it should be carried on in the interests of the social condition of the people of this country. (Applause). Mr. T. E. Hallsworth seconded the resolution. Mr. W. Chandos Wilson also spoke, and the resolu- tion was carried. In the evening there was a crowded meeting in the Free Trade Hall when addresses were delivered by Mr. T. W. Russell, M.P., who presided. Mr. Harry Nuttall, M.P. and Mr. Philip Snowden, M.P. A resolution was passed thanking the Government for their determina- tion to press forward the Licensing B 11 and expressing the hope that it would soon become law.

WARRINGTON.

Wyciffe P.S.A. A welltattended meeting of the Wycliffe Society was held on Sunday. Mr. Joseph Owen presided. Mr. J. Bailey gave the address upon the raising of Jairns's daughter by Jesus. Mr. William Broadhurst also took part in the meeting. Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ, and rendered an organ solo. Four new members received a hearty welcome. Disturbing-Church-street. Thomas Hickman of Dakin's-yard, was summoned for creating a disturbance in Church-street on October 3rd.— He pleaded not guilty.—P.O. Clough stated that I defendant's father informed him that defendant had been creating a disturbance at his house and had knocked two panels of a door out. While he was con- ' versing with the father, defendant was making a scene in Church-street, using filthy language.—Defendant said he was not creating a disturbance at all and that the door gave way owing to a" putrified " hinge. He was fined ss. Bewsey-road Wesley Guild. The members of this guild held their first devotional meeting of the winter session on Monday, when the Rev. E. B Keeling presided over a good attendance. Several of the members took part in the service, announcing hymns, praying and reading lessons, gr. G. B. Keeling gave a paper on " Amos," who, lie said, was a proof that one could live a pure and holy life in the midst of bad surrounding. Mr. Keeling was heartily thanked for his paper, on the proposition of Mr. William Healey, the secretary, seconded by Messrs. Mitchell and Corbett. Miss Jones sang a solo. " HERALD" KITCHEN RANGES and " PEVERIL " FIRE GRATES are ahead of all others. Inspection invited.—J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Rath of Mrs. J. C. Thompson. Many of our readers will deeply regret to hear of the death of Mrs. Thompson, widow of the late Mr. J. Christmas Thompson (headmaster of the School of Art from 1855 to MK, who passed away at her residence, 17, 80ld-street, on Saturday morning. She was taken ill on her return from the Lake district about three months ago,and never subsequently left the house. She had been attended by Dr. Joseph. The cause of death was senile decay. The deceased lady who was highly cultured, will be much missed by a large circle of friends. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Monday, the Rev. E C. E. Carleton' vicar of Holy Trinity Church, Which the deceased lady had attended for many years, officiating. "Weary Workers." The Rev. Canon Stevenson in the course of his sermon at St. Paul's Church, on Sunday morning, had something to say Amt the weary workers who by means of the railway, the motor car, the motor cycle and the bicycle must have rest, refreshment, change and pleasure on the Lord's Day. They saw some of these weary workers at times taking their pleasures, and he should think it would have been very much better for them to have done something else as the manner in which they spent the day could not do them much good. They seemed to forget that there were other weary workers who were being robbed of their rest. They saw the poor, weary, worn out old horse who had worked six days in the week trying to get along with his great load, and they saw the poor driver who had been out very late on a Saturday night having to work again on Sunday. And they had to think of the army of people who, in one way or other, were robbed—and he used the word advisedly—of their Sabbath rest in order that the weary workers might rest themselves after their own fashion forgetful of others.

Warrington Women's Circle, Golborne-street. The meeting of this circle on Sunday was presided over by Mrs. Broadhurst and amongst others who assisted were Mrs. J. S. Hughes, the Misses Bates and Miss Rose Tynan. The address was given by Mrs. John Yonge, who took as her subject the words "Kings and Priests." She gave an excellent lesson on the duties of wives and mothers, and showed that it was the duty of every woman to rule her own home, and guide the religious life of her children. Obedience and the habit of prayer for and with the children was especially emphasized. Miss Maggie Heywood sang "The coming of the King" and the choir gave" Let the S tviour in," Misses Bather and Burney taking the duet. Miss Shaw contributed two recitations. The anniversary serdice in connection with this Society was held at gt: Paul's Church on Thursday evening, when the Bishop of Liverpool (Dr. Chavasse) preached to a-fairly large congregation. Taking for his text the words "Not by might nor by power, but by my Spirit saith the Lord of Hosts. (Zechariah iv., 6.), his Lordship made a strong plea for unity in Church work. He condemned the jealousy which unfortunately sometimes existed in Churches and Sunday schools, and the bad feeling which was apt to be engendered between sections who thought differently within the Church. He urged all to work together in love, relying upon the Rower of the Spirit of God, and then the Kingdom of God would make much greater progress in their midst. LEGS MIIIRN OUT BY DISEASE.

Wears Starting front Small Sora Cured by Zam-Btik.

Constant standing weakens the legs and frequently brings on ulcers, varicose veins, etc., which are the more difficult to cure if the whob limb is in a weakened condition. How wonderfully beneficial Zani-Buk is in such cases is shown in the experience of Mrs. Emma Masters, of 12, Union-street, Smeth- wick, Birmingham. "I am 62 years of age," Mrs. Masters told a "Birmingham Echo" reporter. "Shortly • after my husband's death, twelve years ago, a slight crack appeared on my foot, just below the ankle bone, which caused me great pain. "The doctor, after examination, said it was due to extreme weakness, and that I was on my legs too much. He gave me ointment and medicine, and told me I was to give my legs as mach rest as possible. As far as I could I obeyed his instructions, but the wound grew larger and larger. The wound was always discharging, and the surrounding flesh was inflamed and very painful. My daughter persuaded me to consult a lady doctor, who told me my legs were absolutely worn oat. I had been suffering then for three years, and for another seven years I went on enduring agonies, trying all kinds of lotions and ointments without success.

"A short time ago a friend visited me and prevailed upon me to try Zam-Buk. After dressing the wound a few times with the balm I felt relief from the pain. ZainDuk was cooling and soothing, and with each dressing my ankle got stronger and stronger. The wound gradually got less in size, the inflammation died away, and the discharge ceased. Constant applications of Zam-Buk at length completely healed the wound and covered the place with new healthy skin.

" My leg is now stronger than it has been for years, and since Zam-Buk cured the ulcer there has not been the slightest sign of another outbreak."

===== 0002601_19081017_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LICENSING BILL.

U.K A. Meetings in Manchester.

"A memorable year " Mr. Lief Jones, M.P., presid- ing at the Annual General Council of the United Kingdom Alliance, at Manchester, on Tuesday, called the past year, and said its passing found the Alliance with unbroken courage and quickened enthusiasm. The Government Licensing Bill, in its Local Option pro- visions, embodied the policy of the Alliance, as pressed on the Prime Minister before the Bill as introduced. The time limit was held to be too long, and they would not have any extension of it. The Bill needed strengthening in regard to grocers' licences, Sunday closing, exclusion of Children, closing on election days, and clubs; but the temperance party was firmly united for the Bill, the studied moderation of which had not prevented a wild outcry of interested opposition. The Bill had behind it the splendid unity of the temperance foroes, and also all organisations that make for the uplifting of the people. Mr. Leif Jones was re-elected president. Subsequent speakers included the Right Hon. T. W. Russell, M.P., Mr. T. H. Sloan, M.P., Mr. Charles Roberts, M.P., Sir George White, M.P., the Hon. Geoffrey Howard, M.P., Sir Wilfrid La wson, Bart., Rev. Canon Hicks,M.A., Hon. A. Wilmot, M .L.A. (Cape Colony), Rev. Joseph Cocker, of New Zealand, and Rev. D. V. Lucas, of Toronto. The Government Licensing Bill was the chief topic of every speech. AR were warmly applauded, and the greatest unity and enthusiasm were shown in support of the Bill. The ,principal resolutions passed cordially thanked the Prime Minister and the Government for courageous statesmanship in their oonduot of the Licensing Bill, and specially for the three-fold' inclu- sion of Local Option, and rejoiced in the support of the Labour party, of many notable Unionists, the Free Churches, and a majority of the Bishops. Further resolutions protested against any extension of the time limit held to be already excessive; called for strengtheningi amendments, and urg ed persistent exer- tions n support of the Bill. Vigorous addresses were also given by speaker* from New Zealand and Canada, claiming great success of Local Option in those colonies and other countries. THE ANNUAL PUBLIC MEETING.

It was an immense gathering in the Free Trade Hall, at night which welcomed Mr. Winston Churchill, ALP., President of the Board of Trade, when he took the chair, and as many people waited outside as gained admission. Few, if any preceding annual meetings. have displayed such capacity fer enthusiasm, and probably no chairman ever had a greater reception Mr. Churchill won tremendous cheering when he attri- buted the present advanced position of temperance reform to the fire kept burning by the extremists, and by declaring the Government, Licensing Bill would clear the way to full and effective popular control, and also that this was no time for compromise. Mr. Leif Jones, M.P., moved a tesolution, which was passed, thanking the Government for the Bill, and recognising great support accorded to it by all progres- sive forces. Mr. Jones eulogised Mr. Churchill as being in his right place as chairmen. Right Hon. T. W. Russell, M.P. declared it would pay the country if the Bill were carried, even though they lost their Liberal majority, and gave reasoned argument why the Lords should not reject the measure. Sir John Ciorst, K.C. agreed that the drink traffic was a most gigantic evil. It hindered all social reform, caused poverty, and unemployment, and inflicted fright- ful misery and awful cruelty on children. He hoped the Government would stick to their guns, and they would carry their measure with full 'approval of the British people. Hon. E. S. Montagu, said that if the Bill were beaten England would be the poorer; how could this country fulfil her imperial mission if her people were governed by a trade? Stirring addresses were also given by Sir George White, ALP., Sir Wilfrid Lawson, Bart.,Mr. C. Duncan, M.P., Rev. Dr. Brook, and Canon Hicks. A crowded overflow meeting was held in the Friends' Meeting Hous, at which addressee were delivered by Mr. D. J. Shackleton, IV.P., who presided, Mr. Churchill, Mr. Charles Duncan, M.P., Mr. T. H. Sloan, M.P., and others. The hall proved much too small for the vast crowds who were anxious to gain admittance, and similar resolutions to those adopted at the Free Trode Hall meeting, were unanimously carried. Mr. Churchill got a great reception at this meeting, and, in reply to several suffragists who had made interruptions during his speech, Mr. Chnithill, amidst much.applause, said the temperance movement would stand to gain if women only had the vote, as the drink traffics affected the home, the children, and the best interests of the nati,

SYRUP

Sy DEAR CHILDREN, You will find a. further selection et essays in this week's paper, and I shall print another lot next week, and then the Competi- tion will be absolutely closed, and the prizes awarded. I am very pleased that the subject was suggested by one of my members, as in no other Competition has such interest been shown. Now, as October is drawing on, I wish to make arrangements for our Christmas Com- petitions. I want my members to send me sug- gestions, Just sit down and think what you can easily do yourself, and then write me. I want to have Competitions that all of you can enter for, and not those that apply to a few. For instance, painting could be selected, but I am sure all of you cannot paint, and, conse- quently, marTy would be debarred from enter- ing. Think about it, and send on your sug- gestions during the next week or two. I shall not forget to send a little present to the senders of the accepted ideas. With love, UNCLE NED. KINDNESS TO ,ANIMALS. '(Continued from last week.)

The donkey, which is sometimes called stupid by ignorant people, is a useful animal. They call him stupid because he is obstinate, but obstinacy is caused through ill-treatment. When a donkey has a heavy load to draw, the master should encourage it with coaxing words and give it as much help as possible. In one case the donkey has to suffer for the wanton pleasure of human beings. For instance, at holiday times one of the amusements is that of a donkey running up and down a road with a person on its back, followed by a man or a boy flourishing a stick, which he constantly brings down on the poor creature's back. This, if carried on to too great an extent, causes great pain and suffering on the part of the animal. It is a very common sight in the streets of London to see a man with a per- forming bear. Now, the question is, How are they taught to do it? People say that bricks are heated, and the victim forced to stand on them. Naturally, the bear would not keep still, but would at Once begin to dance about. If this is done, several times, the animal be- comes used to the sensation, and when it is taken into the streets it does the same action. These performances are contrary to the laws of nature', and should not be allowed. The men train them in order to obtain money, so that they shall not have to work themselves. We can discontinue this by refusing to give the men money.

All of us know that a cat arid her kittens affords us a very amusing sight. If you put a saucer of milk on the ground, and ,call the cat and her young ones, the mother will make a noise, Meaning the young ones to have some first. When she thinks they have had suffi- dent, she will_then drink herself. There are many kinds of anitials and many classes. Another name for a domestic animal is an ani- mal which is kept in the house kr household purposes, such as a dog. Some dogs are kept as pets, others as house protectors. They are very useful, as well as being domestic. Dogs are divided into many (ilasses, according to their breed, some being more valuable than others. There is a difference between domes- tic and useful animals. For, instance, a sheep 'is a useful animal, but not domestic. From it we obtain wool, and some are killed for their meat, which is called mutton. People do not care for noxious or mischievous animals,- such as the monkey. The monkey, which greatly resembles the human being, with the excep- tion of its tail, is very destructive, destroying anything that comes within its reach. They range foreign countries, and are very frolic- some little creatures when at liberty; In. the London streets it is not an unusual sight to see a little Italian boy leading one of 'these ,little animals by a long chain. We ought to be just- as kind to these animals as we are to the horse or dog; although they are so very mischievous. A wild animal is not at all 'what we should call civilised. Their coats are generally the colour of their surroundings. The tiger, for example; with its striped coat, matches the long grass of the jungle in which it makes its home. The snake is another ex- ample of a wild animal, although it is really a reptile. It has a very long, green body, which is encircled by black rings. Both the tiger and snake can, therefore, make themselves in- visible, becanse they are coloured accordinL to their surroundings. These animals are very hostile to human beings, and in olden times, when there were so few skilled doctors, they often used to be the cause of their death. The following extract shows that animals have all the gifts of Nimair beings, although•in smaller quantities, and are in no way different to OUT-

The same force fashioned the sparrow That fashioned the, man, the king,

The God of the whole gave feeling

To furred and feathered -thing;

And I am_ ray_ brother's .keeper,

And I Vain fight his fight,

, And speak a word for beast and bird, _ _ _ . . . .

Till the world doth set things right. VIOLET SANDY. Servant (to mistress): " Where shall I take this prescription to, mum'? " Mrs. Sharpeye Mastless): " Anywhere, except Pillbox and Co.'s. Their goods are not fresh. I bought a postage stamp of them yesterday, and it was last year's issue." LOIIIH WATSON.

KINDNESS TO ANIMALS.

We show our kindness to animals by keeping them from all cruelty we can privent, or by attending regularly to their wants when they are entirely dependent upon us. If we look very carefully at either animals or insects, and see how beautifully they are made: I think we should often stop this cruelty to poor dumb animals when we -see it.. In Japan and Italy, the animals are kindly taken care of, and people send money, that these homes for animals should not have to be given up. Also, in England, societies are being formed for the protection of animals. The horse is a very valuable animal, and faith- ful to man. Its fondnese to those who look after it should be regarded, and a great "deal more care bestowed on it. By el-Ll:wing your kindness to animals, I Mean, to study its habits, and giv it food that is necessary for it. The sheep is a very useful animal, and it seems very cruel to cut off the sheep's wool, but, instead of being cruel, it is a kindness to them, for .in summer, when it is so hot, the sheep's wool is a great trial to it, and its wool is needed to be made into woollen clothes. Dogs are very knowing animals, and seem to understand what we say to them. There arc some animals that I have not said anything about, because I cannot see the U—se of them- There are black beetles, frogs, toads, mice, and others that I can only say one thing about, and that is that God created them, so that they must be made for some pmpose, so they look to us for protection: If clitharen are allowed to practise cruelty when they are young, they grow up hard-hearted men and women. Kindness to animals is a very inl- p'ortant thing; it is everybody's duty to be kind to animals, because they are not gifted with speech as we are, and, with kindness. we maY train them to love us, and come at our com- mand. In England, as it is in every civilise(' country, any one who is found treating animals with cruelty is punished. If God loves the animals and insects, we ought to make some good use of them. In Great Britain the kind- ness to animals is much better. I must now finish my essay, and I hope that cruelty wig soon be done away with, and kindness reign: in its place. 001,1STAlicE ALICE BLISSITT.

TO WARRINGTON FROSI

POSTAL AND SAY NU BANK REGULATIONS

For further particulars see "Post Office Guide," at any Post Office. POSTAGE STAM PS.

Books containing 12 penny, and 23 halfpenny stamps are now on sale at each post office. Price 2s. COT

INLAND CORRESPOiiDENCE.

The term "Inland Correspondence " means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom,. Including the Isle of Man and the Channel Islands. " Inland Correspondence" is dirided into the following classes : (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) Postcards (e) Parcels (c) Halfpenny packets - Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation.. being that of size.

LETTEIt POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows : Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... Id. For every additionai.2. 0z5.... t.: ... id. •

' A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufficiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one foot in depth. POST-CARDS.

The prepaid rate Of postage On every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 5+ inches in length by 3+ inches in width, or be less than 3+ inches in length by 21 inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and finely-wide) bearing the address may now be affixed to the address side of card. If any of the rules relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. HALFPENNY PACKET POST.

The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 20z. in weight. Printed matter, &c., not in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST.

The prepaid ra-te.of-pc-iiiitie on l'iegistered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is fd. Newspapers not registered at the General Post Office are treated in the post like any other printed matter. • PARCEL POST. The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: For a parcel ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 exceeding 1 lb. 2 lb. 0 4 f,ç ys 2 lb. 3 lb. 0 5 3 lb. if 5 lb. 0 6 sth. 71b. 0 7 „ 7 lb. 8 lb. 0 8 8 lb • 9 lb. 0 9 If „ 9 lb. „ 10 lb. 0 10 ff 10 lb. „ 11 lb. 011 "No parcel may exceed 111 b. in weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel is : Greatestlength... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. ' For example—A parcel measuring 3ft. 6in, in its longest dimensions as much as 2ft. 6in. round its thickest part; a short parcel may be thicker ' • thus, if it measure no more than 3ft. in length, it may measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid. Mark each parcel" Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of.a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving. PARCELS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL). Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similar to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- • ments the contents must be declared. The cnstotna charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid. MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a id. can be contnined in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 2d. ; £3, Sd.; £lO, 4d.; £2O, 6d. ; £3O, Bd. ; £4O, 10d. By paying an additional 2d. aud cost of the telegram of advice, a money order can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order i may be issued for places abroad varies n each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. ; £2, 6d. ; and above that, 3d. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A fee of 6d. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS. - ,

- These are obtainable at any money order office in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can be paid into, a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by 6d., from 6d. to £1 Is. The poundage fees are up to 2s. 6d.; id. up to 155.; lid. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- maater-General will entertain no further claim after an.order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to makeup odd sums.
