TELEGRAMS (IN-LAND)!

_ The Postmaster-General possesses4lmost entirely, the privilege of transmitting,collecting and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. The charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of 6d. which covers 12 words, and an additional L-d. /or each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a sub-office then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is' made from the office door. All charges are, as far as possible prepared by affixing stamp l to telegram form, Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, but sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment Is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or handtd to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the- nee rest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams banded in after the usual hours. The cpst of registering an abbrevi- ated addresq, which must consist of two words, is £1 Is. TELEGRAMS (FOREIGN AND COLONTAL) Must be written on special forms and are accepted in the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS SAM These are established at all money order offices. In ordinary cases the annual deposit must not exceed £5O, and the total amount £2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children under seven years of age cannot be withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received 'at a time is Is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps affixed to a special form. Each depositor receives a bank book in which the amount of each deposit should be entered at the time of paying in. If the deposit is £1 or over; a spacial acknowledgment is sent from the head London office. Interest is allowed at the rate of 21 per cent. Any amount up to and including £1 may be withdrawn without previous notice, on presenting a bank book. The bank book should be sent each year, postage free, on the anniversary of the date when the account was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Bank Department for purposes of checking and adding interest. The withdrawal of money can bee xpedited by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost of telegrams., P.O. TELEPHONE AND TRUNK LEkTE CHARGES. The fees for the use of the Trunk lines are as follows: For 25 miles or under ... 3d. ,• 50 „ 11 ft 75 ft ff •.• ..• ... 9d. „ 100 „ every a"ddition"al 40 miles or fraction thereof... ... _ 63._

Between the hours of 7 p.m. and 7a re • . minutes conversation is allowed for the same price as three minutes between 7 a.m. and 7 p.m., and a reduced charge is made for three minute calls. -- MOTOR CAR AND CYCLE REGULATIONS.

Each one must be registered with the council of a county orkcounty borough, the fee being £1 bored motor car, and ss. for a motor cycle. A register a number is assigned to each, which it is an offence not to keep affixed whoa driving. Each driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from, the county authorities, which may be endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other effences. The highest speed at which a car may be driven on the publio highways is 20 miles an hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions. It is an offence under the 9fotor Car Act (1903), to drive a car in such a way on a public highway as to be dargerous to the public, having regard to all the circumstances of the case. In the event of an accident to any person due to be presence of the motor car on the road, the river must supply his name and addrEss, and the twner and number of the car, under heavy penal- odie The driver must produceh is licence at the sequest of a police constable.

, RUGBY FOOTBALL.

RESULTS OF SATURDAY'S MATCHES. Goal of any description 2 points. Try 3 points. Australians

THE NORTHERN RUGBY LEAGUE.

G. T. P. G. T. 1,.* *Salford *Dewsbury 1 1 5 *Hull 0 0 0 Broughton R. 5 319 St. .Helens 0 3 9 Swinton Warrington 8 4 28 *Merthyr Tydvil 3 2 12 Runcorn *Barry 014 ham 2 416 Leigh 1 0 2. Wigan *Ebbw Vale Warrington's Fine Win at Merthyr.

Warrington went cn their Welsh tour last week end, and were favoured with capital weather, the trip being much enjoyed. The opening match was at Merthyr T.ydvil on Saturday and Warrington scored a handsome victory before 4,000 people. The game was the Lest seen there for years,and Warrington's display of the finer points of the game was much admired. The team was as follows: Tilley, full Lack ; Fish, Dickenson.Taylor and Cartwright, threeqnarters ; Brooks and Jenkins. half-backs; A. Boardman, P. Boardman,Dow ell, Naylor, Belton and Hewitt forwards. Referee, Mr. W. W. Taylor. Warrington opened in vigorous style and after three minutes' play Dickenson threaded his way through the defence and scored a nice try, which Fish improved. Soon after, the captain dropped a smart goal—a some- what unusual feat for him. The home side were guilty of general rule infringements and Fish kicked three penalty goals in succession. A mistake by Merthyr enabled Naylor to take advan- tage, and Fish again improved the try. Merthyr atiuggled hard, and success at last attended their efforts, for splendid passing between Smith, Paddison, and Bevan resulted in the last-named scoring a try, which James Improved, the interval score beiug —Warrington, 18 _points ; Merthyr, f. points. On the second half play was more even, and James reduced his side's deficit by placing a good penalty goal. Coxineadow took advantage of an opening made by Rees, and as James added the goal Warrington's lead was reduced to half-a-dozen points. Fish and Dickinson, however, in the last quarter gave a remarkable display, and the wing player scored two more tries, which he also improved. Merthyr attacked strongly towards the finish, but the Warrington backs defended well, and the Lancastrians won with 18 ,poiats to spare. Final : Warrington, 8 goals 4 tries (28 points) ; Merthyr, 3 [goals 2 tries (12 points).

I - A DRAW AT EBBW VALE. • On Monday, Warrington appeared at Ebbw Vale and put the same team in the field as on Saturday. Boardman scored two tries in the first half and Fish added goals in each case. Saunders kicked a goal for the home team. At half-time Warrington led by 10 points to 2. In the second half Llewellyn scored a try aud Saunders kicked a goal for Ebbw Vale. Warrington held the lead until near the end, when Dickenson was carried off injured. Davies then scored an equalising try far Ebbw Vale, the match ending in a draw of ten points each. RUNCORN v. TREHERBERT. This match was played at Treherbert on Saturday before a fair attendance. Shortly after the kick-off, passing between Walker Hughes, and Deakin ended in the latter scoring for Walker, the goal-kick failing. Although Treherbert were now having as much of the play as Runcorn, Flannigan fielding a poor kick by F ranc' is passed to Padbury, who scored another uncon- verted try. Immediately afterwards Walker charged down the home captain's kick for Unsworth to score. In the second half Walker and Deakin scored for Runcorn, and Fitzgerald kicked a 400 l for Treherbert. The prominent players for the visitors were Deakin, Padbury, Walker, and Harrison, and for Treherbert . Fitzgerald! J. Thomas, and Came. Just before the finish Harrison was injured, and was taken off. Result: —Runcorn, 5 tries (15 points), Treherbert, 1 goal (2 points).

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

BALLOONS AND RIFLE SHOTS.

Experiments have been made in Berlin to ascertain the height at which a ballopn may be considered immune from hostile rifle shots. Captive balloons were sent up, and _they were shot at from angles of from 154:1eg. to 45deg. Balloons travelling at a height of from 600 to 2,000 yards could be hit only once out of six shots, while they were absolutely safe at a height of 3,000 yards. Even when struck, the damage to the gas bag was so email that the balloon was able to continue its journey for hours before the escaping gas made a landing necessary.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
WATER STERILISED AT THE TAP.

Ozone is one of our most powerful oxi- disers, and Cltambers's Journal mentions that advantage has been taken of this fact in a novel manner in France. Drinking-water, though passed through many filtering and purifying operations, is yet extensively in- fested with micro-organisms. This latest de- velopment is the introduction of a certain quantity of the gas into the water at the tap, so that when drawn the liquid is to all intents and purposes perfectly sterile. The appa- ratus is very simple, and inexpensive to in- stall, and is economical in operation.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SHAPE OF THE SKY.

What is the apparent form of the vault of the sky? There is probably no one to whose eyes it seems a true hemisphere, with the zenith appearing as distant as the horizon. At sea, or in a flat country, the seeming greater distance of the horizon is best shewn. A well-known professor, in discussing this subject, reaches the conclusion that- the form of the vault, in vertical section,. is that of the segment of a circle, the arc of which subtends at the centre an angle of the order of 40kleg. If the reader will draw such a segment, he may be surprised by the amount of flattening which is thus ascribed to the sky. From this optical illusion many curious effects arise, such as the seeming increased Magnitude of the sun and moon when near the horizon. and the apparently oval' forms of halos and eoronas seen at low altitudes.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
A TIMBER-MEASURING MACHINE.

A resident of Louisiana, U.S.A., has put upon the market a patented machine which 'a designed to measure timber, and not only measures it, but records the measurements in a way that •is much quicker than the old system, and 'vastly more! accurate The machine has a chute through which the stock- boards or planks are gassed. As they pass through, says the Timber News, they touch a revolvable shaft, which as it turns registers the distance it covers. Each foot is recorded upon an appliance attached to the side, and It is only necessary to consult this register to find how many feet of lumber have passed through the machine. The possibility of mis- takes in measurements is practically elimi- nated, and the stock can be measured by any person, whether he has a knowledge of figures or not.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
ABSOLUTE ZERO.

The recent liquefaction of helium has called special attention to the question of low temperature in, general, and to absolute zero in particular. The suggestion of an ab- solute zero was made by Lord Kelvin in 1848, and the conception arose in this way. It was found that the pressure of a gas de- pended on its temperature according to a certain ,law. Calculation shevved that if this law held universally, then, at a temperature or 273 deg. below the zero of a Centigrade thermometer, a gas would have no pressure ar. all. Now, according to the Kinetic theory of gases, the pressure exerted by them is due to the rapid movement of their molecules, which under ordinary conditions is at the rate of about two and a-half miles a minute. Hence at 273 deg. bqlow zero there would be no movement of the molecules, and hence no heat. Thus the point of zero pressure is also the point of zero heat.

LOWEST TEMPERATURE REACHED.

Recent experimental research has brought

physicists into exciting proximity to the goal of absolute . zero. Liquid hydrogen gives a temperature only 20deg. above it. Liquid helium, again, gives us a temperature of 4•sdeg. absolute, or -. 268'5deg. below Odeg. Cent. .By allowing helium to boil at a low pressure, 3deg,, above absolute zero were reached. This is probably the lowest tem- perature yet attained. It has been suggested that if one of the rarer gases such as the coronium, known to exist in the furthest atmosphere of the sun, could be liquefied, still lower temperatures might • be reached. And when this desired goal of 273cleg. below Odeg. Cent. is attained, two things, the Globe remarks, seem possible. Either -the absolute absence of heat will be found to be still fur- ther off, or the total cessation of movement may mean the annihilation of matter.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW ERA OF SCIENCE.

"This is a scientific period- as remarkable as that in which Sir Isaac Newton discovered the laws ' of gravity;" says -Mr. Waldemar Kaempffert, of the Sciedific American'.

"I am speaking now of pure, not applied, science," he continues. "We are living in what may be called a state of scientific tran- sition. Discoveries in the radio-active sub- stantes, recently or now being made, such as those relating to the degradation of copper ta lithium and the change of radium to helium; have disturbed all previous conceptions and lead us to new theories regarding matter. "What is 'matter? The old philosophers and even the chemists of but a decade ago could find no very satisfactory definition. The Modern physicist, aided by experimental evidence with a Crookes tube and radio-ac- tive substances, is at least

===== 0002601_19081017_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
GROPING NEARER THE TRUTH.

He is quite convincer) at least, that the atom, small as it may be to the popular mind, is gigantic compared with what it ought to be. We find that the leading spirits in the re- vision of our chemistry, men like Sir 0. Lodge, Sir W. Crookes, and Professor J. J. Thomson, have substituted for the old atom a new and very much smaller unit called the 'electron.' According to the new views, atoms are composed of electrons. In size the electron bears the same relation to an atom that a baseball bears to the earth. Or, as Sir Oliver Lodge puts it, if a hydrogen atom were magnified to the size of a church an electron would be the equivalent of a speck a dust in that church.

"According to the old atomic theory, there were seventy odd bits of primorial matter, snch as iron, zinc, lead, gold; copper, and arsenic—bits of matter which were sup- posed to be

ABSOLUTELY UNCHANGEABLE. When Sir W. Ramsay discovered radium, which was considered an element, changed into helium, still another element, and that by means of radium emanation copper (an element) could be degraded to , lithium (another element), what. becomes of this sup- posed immutability of the atom? The elec- tron theory explains away these difficulties. There is but one kind of .matter, according to the newer views. If we arrange electrons in, one way and give them a certain motion we obtain copper; change that arrangement of electrons, or their motion, or perhaps their number, and we obtain lithium. "Thus the new science derives all sub- stances from one primeval variety of matter. In other words, the TRANSKUTATION OF MATTER,

the dream of medireval alchemy, is realised in our own time. We have not succeeded in converting baser metals into gold, but we have succeeded in changing one element to another, which is just as startling from a scientific view-point. Sir Oliver Lodge and Sir William Crookes have not hesitated to bridge the chasm between experimental science and metaphysics. They have boldly built on a system of Physics based partly upon the experimental evidence of the labo- ratory, partly upon sheer speculation, with the result that they have given us perhaps the most beautiful and simple conception of the nuivcrsa. that mankind has ever received."

===== 0002601_19081017_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====

WARRINGTON "A" V. PEDIRRRTON.—Another large crowd assembled at Wilderipool on Saturday, and had the satisfaction of witnessing a hard game end in a victory for the " Wirepullers." Bradshaw was an absentee from the home side. In the first half the visitors were quite the equal of the home set, and the vigorous work of the forwards more than counter. balanced the smart work of the Warrington back division. Kay was to the fore in repeatedly opening out the game, and it was not until the near approach of the interval that a score came, Daintith tiking the ball over near the Rests and Forster kicking a goal. With a lead of five points Warrington entered upon the second moiety full of confidence and Daintith early on scored after flue passing. Lander next brOke through, closely followed by Kaye, this last try being a particularly fine single-handed effort., Harries next crossed, and Warrington maintained the advantage to the close, winning by 2 goals, 6 tries, 22 points to nil.

QUESTIONS FOR TARIFF REFORINERS.I , -- 1.-If British Trade is unprofitable, and we are being ruined by free imports, how is it that the business profits assessed to Income Tax are always rising rapidly P

The last published Report of the Inland Revenue Commissioners contains a complete answer to the assertion that British trade is unprofitable. Dealing with the assessments to Income Tax, it shows for the period 1808-190; : PROFITS FROM BUSINESSES, CONCERNS, PROFESSIONS, AND EMPLOYMENTS. al Yew 0. 1d Mitch 31. 1898 ... ••. 1899 ... 1900 ... •••

It can hardly be alleged, in face of these accom- plished facts, that British trade is "unprofitable." A growth of £118,000,000 or 2:5 per cent. in only nine years is exceedingly satisfactory. If we take the record further back we find that in 1809 prgfits were only .Z 173,000,090 ; in. 1878, 1272,000000 ; in 1395, £310,000,000. The importance of these records lies in the fact that they relate to both home and export trade, not to the latter only. G. C.

Friendly Societies and the Pensions'

Committee.

Sir,—The different orders of friendly societies in Warrington have just held their quarterly meetings and the majority have passed resolutions regretting that the Town Council had not taken advantage of the privilege granted under the act to co-opt a member or members of friendly societies on the Old Age Pension Committee. As the Council have failed to recognise the just claims of friendly societies it is for us now to take action and endeavour to nominate and secure election of friendly society members at the forthcoming November municipal elections and thus secure direct representation on the Old Age Pension Committee. If friendly societies are earnest in their protesta- tion I shall be pleased to receive requests to hold a public meeting to consider the whole question.—Yours faithfany,

ROBERT PERDUE, District Treasurer, Warrington Equalized Order of Druids. 23, Buttermarket-street, Warrington, October 16th, DOS.

Education Committee and Extravagance. Sir,—So,much has been said,especially of late,about the " irresponsible and extravagent " co-opted members of the above, that perhaps a few facts regarding them may not be out of place. The Education Bill of 1002 contained the principle of co-option and conferred equal administrative powers upon representative and co-opted members. In 1903, the Act became operative in Warrington, and the Borough Council elected the Education Authority, consisting of 21 representative and 16 co-opted mem- bers. It will be apparent to all that Town Council representatives are in the majority, and thus have the power to control. If the Authority's policy has been an extravagant one (and I for one maintain it has not), then the responsibility rests upon the representative members, for not checking same. But further more, the Town Council can ;reject all resolutions of a financial nature that come before them, upon the minutes of the Education Committee. It will thus be seen that there is a counter cheek upon extravagance. The work of the authority, both in the sub-com- mittees and the general body,devolves upon the co-opted members, as the attendance of many of the representa- tive members is so poor. Some scarcely ever put in an appearance, whilst others remain but a short time. I admit they have other municipal duties to perform, but maintain that complaints that are so unjustifiable should not be made against those who are doing the work. The average attendance ef representative members is 58'3, and that of co-opted members 75,1 for this year, thus giving proof of my assertion. Many of the co-opted members are selected by and are responsible to a much larger electorate than some of the representative members ; especially does this apply to Aldermen. I trust, Sir, that the time will soon come when the Education Authorities will be elected by the electors of the town, and for educational purposes only.—Yours truly, EDWIN PLINSTON.

The November Elections.

Sir,—The Ist of November approaches and with it the usual flutter in the opposing camps of our political parties in regard to our municipal affairs. Last November two good business men were defeated by representatives of the Trade who were going to improve education, to abolish slum dwellings, and improve the general status of the workingman. Again we have the Trade bringing out their candidates for Municipal honours and I suppose making the same glib promises to be decently interred as soon as the election day is passed I am surprised that the electors can be so easily taken in as it is impossible for the Trade to carry out any reform. The Trade is a stumbling-block to education and it is from the customers of the Trade that our slum population is recruited. I feel certain , that the recent black Sunday in Hyde Park will have conferred very little credit on the Trade and will have shown by the scenes that took place not only the necessity of the Licensing Bill but the necessity of a far more stringent measure of temperance reform to curb and restrain this nefarious and evil trade —a trade which is sapping the life-blood of our nationhood, is destroying the moral 1 fibre of our race, and is leading to political suicide and national bankruptcy. No one who votes for a member of the Trade can be voting for the moral or material welfare of the town.-1 am yours &c.. PHIL SQI:7OD.

Sir,—The Tories are concentrating all the effort and energy they possess in opposing the I.L.P. nominees.l Councillor Broadharst with his usual vigour sets about them in a desperate and violent manner, metaphorically speaking. "Mr. Ball and Mr. Hampson are not Labour men, they are Socialists pure and simple " he informs his audience. lherefore, let us diagnose the elements of what should compose a Labour man. In the first place, Mr. Ball and Mr. Hampson are selected by the Independent Labour Party, which is composed of men of various occupa- tions, but at least DO per cent. of them can be termed workingmen, who, by their accummulated subscrip- tions pay tho election expenses, select their candidates, and work for them at election times without receiving the slightest remuneration. Both Mr. Ball and Mr. Hampson are members and are receiving the support of the Gasworkers and General Labourers' Colon, and both are being supported by the Trades and Labour Council. Councillor Ball has supported all the measures likely to benefit the working classes, amongst which may be mentioned— abolition of fees in Council schools, municipal coal supply, and feeding of necessitous children. If these are not credentials enough to call oneself a Labour man I don't know what the credentials of a Labour man should be. In addition Councillors Steel snd Broad- hurst have been displaying their knowledge, I prefer to call it ignorance, of the principles of Socialism. I challenge Councillor Steel to show .us where in any single proposition of Socialism, it is antagonistic to the teaching of Jesus Christ !—Yours truly, LABOR OMNIA VINCIT.

A School for Anti-Socialists.

Last year the "Standard" commenced a great fight against Socialism. Plenty of ink was spilt to stay the rapid spread of its pernicious doctrines. The Editor appealed to every right-minded Englishman to fight it to the bitter end. Socialism was stripped of its sophisms and sentiment, and the articles of its creed were exposed. It should have been killed but for some unearthly reason it still lives and nourishes. It really ought to die but it won't. The next move to kill the dread disease is, I notice, a school for speakers against it. The Manchester " Guardian" told us the other day that several peers and members of Parliament had visited a "school for anti-Socialists" which has been formed in connection wilh the recently instituted Anti-Socialists Union. Mr. Claud Lowther is chairman of the new organisation, and amongst the patrons of the Union are the Dukes of Westminster and Rutland, Lord Claud „Hamilton, Lord Dorton, Lord Londonderry, Lord

Leconfield, Lord Abinger, and several members of Parliament. The Union proposes to intro- duce into clubs, institutes, and other places trained speakers who have made a special stndy of the case for and against Socialism. Barristers, solicitors, and working men, it is stated, are already among the students at the school, and no speaker will be allowed to represent the Union until he has satisfactorily passed an examination. Another department is for the issue ! of publicathons. Thirdly, an intelligence department I has been established. It is stated that correspondents all over the country will keep this department informed of the progress or decline of Socialism in their particu- lar districts. Later it is proposed to form a circle of writers, sneakers, and M.P.'s who will meet periodically to hold informal debates and exchange ideas and suggestions. One welcomes the school with real gladness ; and to see the names of so many men who work so hard for a living, associated with the movement, should be an inspiration to others. It is rather a pity that the Editor of "The Standard" had not postponed his attack until his soldiers had been better equipped for the battle. To read some of the replies to the questions sent out to prominent public men shows clearly that the school is much needed and will fill, as the advertiser says, a long-felt want. For instance, the Bishop of Croydon, when asked by the Editor to say what he considered to be " the best method of fighting Socialism," replied : "I don't want to fight it. I believe that Socialism is a coming force, and that we must apply ourselves to directing it into wise ways—Christian Socialism, or something in that direction ; but I don't think that it represents the mind of the Church. I feel equally sure that the general principle underlying Socialism is a Christian principle, and that the Church must bend her thoughts more that way." I notice that Mr. W. Peter Rylands is holding an "impartial" inquiry in your contemporary. When the long-drawn-out articles are complete I may have some- thing to ray about them. In the meantime, I would like to emphasize the fact that a school for speakers and writers against socialism has been inaugurated, and I would suggest to Mr. W. Peter Bylands and his confreres that they should take a course of training at the School, and ventilate their profound ignorance of economics before they criticise Socialism.—Yours truly, J 0 UN WOW:MAN.

A PIONEER OF TOTAL]:ABSTINENCE.

Death of Miss Clarke.

Warrington has lost one of its oldest inhabitants and Methodism a valued worker by the death of Miss Elizabeth Clarke who passed away, at her residence, 221, Knutsford-road, on Sunday morning. She was born at Stockton Heath in December, 1318, and was the last member of a worthy and interesting family. Her father the late Mr. William Clarke, who was for over GO years a local preacher and who lived to be SI years of age, became early interested in the temperance move- ment and along with Thomas Eaton, of Stockton Heath, formed what was undoubtedly the first total abstinence society in England. Their meetings were held for several years in the home of William Clarke at Stockton Heath. In 1330 the temperance movement took form under the advocacy of George Harrison Birkett, and a society was established in Stockton Heath. At the first meeting held on April 4th, 1830, Miss Clarke was present and signed the roll as a junior member. She was the sole survivor of those who were present at that historic gathering, and to the end of her life had a clear and vivid remembrance of it. We owe also to her care the preservation of the original roll book and rules of the society. By the aid of these and other documents, Mr. Arthur Mounfield was able some six months ago to prove that Warrington and not Preston was the birth place of the temperance move- ment. In a more recent work, " A Village Centenary," he has given the facts in greater detail and the volume will no doubt be prized as an interesting memorial to William Clarke and his lire work. Miss Clarke was a devoted Wesleyan Methodist and did untiring service for that Church over a long period. As a girl she worshipped in the old chapel in Bank- street, subsequently used as a County Court, and now , serving the purposes of a Ragged Sunday School and Mission. She was an earnest and capable Sunday school teacher and class leader, and for many years conducted a young women's Bible Class in the old school in Buttermarket-street. To the end of her days she maintained her interest in the welfare of the Wesleyan Church, and her work on its behalf may be said to have been the joy of her life. The funeral took place at Hill Cliff° on Wednesday afternoon. The first part of the service was held in Bold-street Church, the Revs. E. Blanshard Keeling and T. Miller officiating. The hymns "Rock of Ages" and "When all Thy mercies" were sung, Mr. F. Hickman presiding at the organ. The Rev. T. MILLER, in the course of an address, said that as one who had recently come to that Church, he felt that it would he better that one who had been acquainted with Miss Clarke for many years should write a few notes. Mr. Moss, of Stockton Heath, undertook -that little duty, and he (Mr. Miller) would read what he had written. Mr. Moss wrote that by the death of Miss Clarke there had been removed from amongst them the oldest mem- ber of the church and one of the very few connecting links between the present and the Methodism of that circuit seventy years ago. She had been a member for seventy-two years, receiving her first ticket as a member on trial on December 7th, 183 G. Like many of the earlier Methodists, she kept a diary and its perusual showed how carefully she watched her inner life. She had few educational and material advantages but she had considerable natural gifts and these improved by mound stimulated spiritual fervour made her a power for good amongst them. As a Sunday school worker, class leader and grayer leader, she rendered long, efficient and fruitful service. She was always ready for every good word and work, but perhaps the two prominent features of her Christian work were her Sunday school class and her society class. She took an intelligent view of Sunday school work and regarded it as one of the most important branches of Christian activity. For many years she walked from Stockton Heath to the old Buttermarket-street school to conduct the young womens class and neither strain of circumstances nor stress of weather prevented her attending. The society class was formed in 1353. The number of members who joined it at different times was 22! and of those 187 were scholars in Buttermarket-street school, a very good proportion and one which if main- tained to-day would be a great help to their church life. The number of times Miss Clarke was present at class meeting was 1239, and in her 50 years as leader she was absent only 15 times. She had been trained in a good school, for her father and mother were devoted members of the Wesleyan Church and her home was one of cheerful Godliness. Her father was for more than fifty years an efficient local preacher, and did yeoman service in his day. They did not gather together, continued Mr. Miller, in the sense of grieving her departure so much as to recognize her long and earnest work for the Church of Christ. They esteemed her for her work's sake. The long day was ti ow over ; she had entered into her rest and was reaping her reward for the services she had rendered in past years. Amongst those present in Bold-street Church or at the graveside, in addition to relatives were : Mr. W. Hickman, Mr. S. Moss, Mr. Geo. Weaver, Mr. J, Holland, Mr. A. T. Lunt, Mr. Reginald Lunt, Mr. A. Mounfield and Mr. J. Whitley. Tha Rev. T. Miller performed the last rites at the graveside.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The _chief feature of the entertlinmont at the Palace and Hippodrome this week has been the Beauty Show for prizes ranging in value from .Z 5 to Mr. Bradbury, the manager, made his debut on Monday evening with an announcement from the stage of the particulars of the competition. Mr. Widdop and his orchestra greeted Hr. Bradbury's appearance with an outburst of melody, the principal theme of which tvas " Put me among the girls." The audience was greatly amused, and Mr. Bradbury could not withhold a smile when the point of the joke reached him. The competition itself afforded much interest to the audience who had 23 samples of British beauty to select from at the first house. Some of them would hardly have qualified for Gaiety Girls, but three were chosen by vote to enter the final contest on Friday evening. Ten competitors entered for the hair competition and some fine "heads " were presented. A. really great turn is given by the Brothers St. Clair, the world's champion bicycle polo and football players. Their tricks on the wheel were amazingly dexterous, while the football match was a real live struggle in charge of a referee. The way the two men wheeled and doubled, shot and dribbled on bicycles was most exciting. A smart parlour turn entitled "Love Germs" is presented by the Smythsons. Unlike so many of its class, the sketch has the merit of originality and the business is bright and an:lush:lg. Pete and Juno are a couple of coloured comedians, whose forte is sand-dancing, at which they are extremely clever. Steve Bartle, the concertina soloist and vocalist, earns enthusiastic encores by his expert handling of the popular instrument. He performs on various sizes of concertina, one of which can be comfortably stewed away in a trousers pocket, and is yet capable of real melody. Annie Heath is a pleasing comedienne and dancer, whose singing and dancing are above the average. There is plenty of dancing this week, and none do better than the Five Broadway Girls, who are excellent exponents of the high-kicking art. One of the troupe appears as the Gibson Girl in the tradi- tional black velvet frock and an amplitude of curves. The biograph pictures are new and interesting, and some tasteful orchestral selections are given daring the interval, and throughout the beauty competition. RESULTS OF THE BEAUTY COMPETITION% The results of the beauty and " hair" competitions were announced last night as follows :—Beauty, 1 Annie Smith, 2 Lillie Richardson, 3 S. Dobson. Hair,

1 Miss Gaskell. Cowelatiort prizes will be given to- night.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAIIIIE.

Next week we are to have a visit from the great Karno troupe of comedians in an entirely new and up-to-date burlesque. " Humming Birds," a musical travesty on the modern music hall, direct from the Oxford and Tivoli, London. The Karla° troupe are amongst the most famous of laughter-makers, and this their latest triumph should prove a great attraction in Warrington. A --most interesting programme will also be contributed to by the following : Winifred Ward, the immaculate boy ; Lillie Hayes, comedienne ; Denaro Brothers, juvenile equilibrists and acrobats ; Dan Whitley, eccentric comedian ; Bains and Reed in their original speciality "Humpsti Butnpsti " ; Cliff and Kean, comedy artistes.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. " By Ovallst."

ANOTHER WELSH INTERNATIONAL SECURED. It was hoped sincerely that Warrington's Welsh tour would be productive of players as well as points, and the hope has been justified by results. Three out of four possible points were obtained, and the signature secured of W. O'Neill of Cardiff, an Inter- national forward and reckoned to be one of the best players in Wales. My readers will reaollect that, a fortnight ago, I said there were hopes of Warrington securing another international forward. O'Neill was the player in question.

===== 0002601_19081017_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
IV. O'NEILL.

A Cardiff correspondent sends me the following : Warrington's latest recruit, William O'Neill of Cardiff, is the cleverest forward Wales has possessed for many years. His forte is his capacity for starting the backs from open play and his club has won many matches through his skill in this respect at times when the forwards have been badly beaten in tight scrummaging. His latest success was at Bristol last Saturday week when three tries resulted directly from his passes. Lie is not a bustling type of forward though he has weight,(l3 stones,lolbs)is fairly fast over a short I distance and tackles thoroughly. In the hurly burly of stern forward play he would not be so serviceable as Dowell, his fellow Welsh International, but he has a keen eye for an opening and is a more skilful dribbler, and in a rush seems always to realize when to pick up and throw to the backs and when to keep the ball a-rolling before the feet.

He is several years older than aiwell, in fact he is entering the thirties, but he has lived a clean life, is practically a teetotaller and has never been over- trained, his habits and his employment keeping him unusually fit. O'Neill as his name signifies is an Irishman, and he is a Catholic. Possibly the absetice of the line-out from the Northern Union rules will rob his play of much of its attractiveness but O'Neill will be an acquisition for reason of his football intelligence.

The news of his resolution to join the Northern Union came as a thunder-clap to the Welsh public as O'Neill appeared to be the last man to make the jump. He has plated for Cardiff since 1807-8 the year of the famous Gwynn Nicholls' captaincy, and for Wales he has played on ten occasions and in all matches last season.

An interview with O'Neill has been published by the " South Wales Daily News," which states : O'Neill was at the smoking concert given by the Cardiff Club last evening to the Australians and to welcome home It. A. Gibbs and J. L. Williams from Australasian tour. It did not leak out that he had joined the professional ranks, but he had consulted some of his friends and taken them into his confi- dence. When told that the fact of his "going over "would be reported in the next morning's newspapers, O'Neill, who had been disinclined to make any public announcement until he leaves Cardiff on Friday, said he would like it to be known that he did not leave his old club in pique, and would have remained an amateur if he could have afforded to ignore the offer made to him. "I go," he said, to better myself. I have a wife and a child, and lam only a working mm. I was offered £lOO down, and I could'nt resist it— indeed I felt at the time and I now feel it too, that I would have failed in my duty to my wife and my young child if I had not taken the chance—it takes a working chap along time to save £lOO you know. It may be all right fer well-to-do people to say they prefer amateur sport—l have been satisfied with that myself until now—but let them put themselves in my position. " My one regret (concluded O'Neill with an exhibition that could only be taken as of genuine feeling) is that I have to leave Cardiff, where I have many friends, and where I have received every kindness from the old club committee, officials and my fellow-players. I had to wrestle with myself before I could put pen to paper to sign myself to go North because of the happy days I have spent in Cardiff, but we—my wife and I—think it was the best thing to do, and I hope my old football friends will try to place themselves in my position before condemning me."

O'Neill has been selected to play to-day in the great match with Wigan at Wilderspool. Warrington will need all the assistance he can give them to check the onward march of the collie's. SOIL I see no reason why Warrington should fear the issue of the match. They never have been frightened of Wigan, and there is no reason why they should begin to quake now. Wigan can be well beaten, as Leigh showed a fort- night ago, and if Warrington rise to the occasion in 'their old style they should have a fair chance of success. Dickenson's injury is a serious blow and quite disorganises the three-quarter line, which will probably be Warrington's weakest point. Determina- tion can make up for a lot of cleverness, and I don't think Cartwright and Lmdir will fail for want of trying.

The selected team is : Tilley, full back ; Fish, Cartwright, Taylor and Landor, three-quarters • Brooks and Jenkins, half backs ; A. Boardman, P. -Boardman, O'Neill, Dowell, Belton and Naylor, forwards.

The " A " team will have a severe task at Wigan, but they have a good side available : Mclntyre, full back; Bradshaw, Oakes, Lloyd and Jordan, three- quarters ; Kay and Daintith, half backs ; Harmer, Hewitt, Fairhurst, Harrison, Bradshaw and Chester, forwards.

It is rather curious that Warrington should have given exhibitions worthy of their palmiest days, both at Merthyr and Ebbw Vale. The spectators at each match said it was the best seen on their ground since the advent of the Northern Union. At Merthyr the Warrington men particularly distinguished them- selves, scoring 28 points to 12. Fish kicked seven goals in succession and scored a couple of tries, while the combination between Dickinson and his wing was brilliant. The pair were repeatedly cheered for clever football.

On Monday Warrington should also have secured full points Irani Ebbw Vale, but for an unfortunate accident to Dickenson. The team scored 10 points to 2 before the interval, and looked certain winners. The Vale forwards caught Dickenson when he had no one to pass to and downed him heavily, with the result that he was led from the field with a cut on the head and a twisted knee. Warrington were thus weakened at a critical stage of the game, and the home team manacled to equalise the scores on the call of time. The scorer was " Dai " Davies, who played full back for the Vale, and followed up a short punt near touch and scrambled over. His old comrades were not quite as pleased as be was. Ebbw Vale have improved a lot since last season, and in Harvey and Higgins they possess a clever pair of half backs.

The Warrington forwards give a good account of themselves in b,th matches, All Boardman leading the pack in fine style. On Monday he had the honour of scoring both Warrington's tries and was easily the best forward on the field. Dickinson's return to form was most gratifying, and it seems the cruellest luck that he should get injured again. No one was more upset than he was, for the lengthy skipper of the team was hoping to lead his side to victory against the men of Wigan to-day.

Most of Warrington's old Welsh players turned up to shake hands on the Monday. It seemed quite like old times to have Dai Davies, Thomas, Shugars, F. Jenkins and Jack Davies standing round. There was general surprise at the appearance of Thomas. He was looking in very poor health and appeared to have lost , a lot of weight. Warrington's position in the League has improved as the result of the three points g lined in Wales, but they have a long way to go before they can view the situation with satisfaction,

Hockey.

Latchford's fixtures last Saturday with Winnington Park produced two rousing games. The first teams met at Latchford and Winnington gained the day by 2-1, although in doing so they must be accounted some- what lucky. The home team showed much improved form all round. Banks scored for Latchford.

At Winnington the home team scored in the first ten minutes but shortly after the visiting forwards ran clean through the opposing defence and Parker equalised. The rest of the game was a keen struggle for the winning goal but none came. A. Knight played remarkably well at centre half for Latchford.

To-day the first eleven are at Weaste with the following team :—W Unsworth, goal; C Plinston, T Dixon, backs; D Travis, J B Wilkinson (capt.), Albinson, halves; F Newell, R Jones, H Scott. E Banks, W Spensley, forwards. Train leaves Central Station 1-55. Referee, Mr. W B Cobb.

The second team are at home to Ashley with the following players :—J Appleton, goal ;- J Wilkinson, W H Pickles (capt.), backs; S B Rumney, A Knight, F Tole, halves; H Booth, F Dunn, H Parker, W H Minards, C Hackney, forwards. Bully-off 3.0 pm. Referee, Mr. Hazy Warburton.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING.

On Saturday last the devotees of this sport were favoured with grand weather, musters were good every- where, and if beginners were encouraged by the warmth the more seasoned runners took matters easily. Sutton Harriers, Parr A.G., and St. Austins formed a pack of 40 to follow a six miles track, the fixture being one of the St. Helens and District League series, under the auspices of the newly formed Association, who for the present season are oonflning themselves to the immediate neighbourhood of St. Helens. The premier club bad matters all their wayaa shown by the r(sult : Sutton, Welding, 1 ; Straw, 2 ; Whittle, 3 ; Greenall, ; Burrows, 5 ; Morris, 6; points 21. Parr A.C. : Harrison, 7 ; Rigby 9; Livingstone, 10; Pimblett, 12 ; Ashton, 12 ; Ellison, 23; points, 83. St. Austins : Connor, 8 ; Meredith, 11; Mercer, 15 ; Parr, 17; Wharton, 28; Lowey, 32 ; points, 111. W. T. Clarke and J. Roberts led a strong pack of Sefton Harriers over about seven miles in the Aigburth district ; P. Pick and R. Body were the most prominent novices.

Liverpool Harriers—once a force to be reckoned with —are evidently bent on improving their position : 3C runners covered about seven miles in the neighbour- hood of Wavertree, a break over the last mile resulting 'as follows :—J. A. Fallow, 1 ; W. Trotter, 2; A. Middleton, 3 ; the novices were promising. Broughton Harriers had 30 out for an enjoyable run which c•mcluded with a mile break between teams picked by the Captain and Secretary, the Captain's team winning by 31 points to 47 ; W. H. Waring was first man home followed by J. H. Murray and W. Greenough. Bolton United Harriers are apparently reviving the sport in their district. They have 250 members 40 of whom ran over an eight mile course. Crewe Harriers started from Oak Farm Hotel, Crewe, and covered about five miles. E. Aspinall (vice-captain), led a pack which included Bennion, Bowyer and Millington, who were whipped in by T. Hossack (captn) ; the most prominent novices were T. Aspinall, W. Edwards and B. Hickton. Bylands W.R.C. Harriers' Sankey, Warrington Wes- leyans and the new club at Stockton Heath continue to have a good muster, but the former considering the facilities they possess might, with advantage, extend their operations amongst the large number of youthful employes who would derive great benefit from a cross country run. Warrington A.C. had a good muster for an easy run over about six miles. W. Unsworth acted as pace and H Holbrook as whip, for a pack comprising nearly all the most prominent of last season's runners. • The novices had a good time and J. Atherton, C. Dakin, S. Holbrook, W. Saepherd and J. Thompson made a promising debut All menibers and intending members are cordially invited fora similar run this afternoon, at three o'clock from the Bridge Inn. Tuesday's run will be over about 2 miles of road ; starting at 8 p.m. , The annual general meeting of the Northern Counties Association will be held at the Queen's Hotel, Promenade, Southport, this eveninig at 6 p.m. prompt. All affiliated clubs are entisled to send two representa- tives ; the agenda comprises several interesting items. " Venue of Championships" is of grert importance from a financial point of view—where the junior clubs are concerned.

QUARTERLY MEETING OF ODDFELLO'NS.

The quarterly meeting of the Warrington District of the Independent Order of Oddfellovvs (Manchester Unity) was held at the " Prince of Wales " Lodge, Pelican lintel, Battermarket-street, on Saturday, under the presidency of Prov. G.M. Lot Brooks. The Secre- tary presented the accounts, which showed that after paying funeral benefits amounting to £BO, there had been placed to reserve £ll3 Os. 9d., making the amount of the funeral fund £3.341 17s. 81c1.—The accounts and the report were adopted, and a vote of thanks was accorded to the auditors.—The next business was the presentation of the handsome regalia, consisting of a silver chain, mounted on ribbon, silver gilt pendant with enamelled arms, and apron and case, which had been generously provided by the Warrington District Children's Burial Society for the use of the Immediate Past Prov. G.M. and his successors. In the absence through illness of P.P.G.M. Adam G. Webster, the presentation was made by P.G. Frederick Taylor. Prov. G.M. Lot Brooks, in accepting the gift, expressed the sincere thanks of the district to the society for their generosity.—Bro. Taylor then proceeded to invest Immediate P.P.G.M. William Holbrook, and spoke very highly of his work as a district officer.—Bro. Holbrook replied in a reminiscent strain.—A cordial vote of thanks was accorded to Bro. Taylor for his services and to the members of the committee of the society who bad supported him, for their attendance, the following being present, viz.:—P.G. Samuel Taylor (vice- president), P.P.G.M. J. H. Norbury, P.P.G.M. Alban Boyle, P.G. William Watson, P.G. George Ashton, P.G. William Houghton, P.G. Thomas Wadsworth and P.G. Dyson ‘Vhitham.—lt is interesting to note that the society has from its formation been a great success, having now a capital of over £B,OOO. and it is in a position to give every advantage in the way of teneflts.—The nest business was the report of the representatives to the Lancashire Conference at Earlestown, which was given by P.P.G.M. T. B. Bradshaw, after which another interesting ceremony was performed—the presentation by the district of a gold merit jewel to P.P.G.M. T. P. Bradshaw for services rendered.—Prov. Gi. M. Lot Brooks made the presentation.—Bro. Bradshaw replied and touched upon several important matters with refer- ence to the working of the district.—The next item had reference to the Town Council, in their election of the Old Age Pensions Committee, not haling deemed it desirable to adopt the recommendation of the Local Government Board to utilize the services of representa- tives of friendly and other societies. The action of the Committee of Management of the district in writing to the Town Clerk in expressing their regret at the course which had been adopted was confirmed.—The meeting next proceeded to receive nominations for the offices of Prov. G.M. and Prov. Prov. D.G.M. G. F. Freeman was nominated for the former position and P.O. T. P. Forbes (" Olive Branch" Lodge) and P.G. A. E. Burdett (" Constitution " Lodge) for the latter.

" MERRIE ENGLAND."

AMATEUR OPERA AT THE THEATRE. More Honours for iiiiMusioal Society.

It has become an agreeable annual task to say nice things about the Musical Society's operatic ventures. This is the sixth year since the Society took the bold and fortunate step of undertaking amateur operatic production in addition to the usual winter concert programme, and one success has followed another with such uniformity that one is inclined to believe that the Society has learned to command success as well as to deserve it. Merit does not invariably get its due reward in this peculiar world, but honours have never yet been withheld from the opera section of the Musical Society. It is evident, therefore, that something must be said for the appreciative and generous temperament of the Warrington public. The whole of this week has seen the Royal Court Theatre, for the first time, given over to the production of amateur opera. In previous I years the Parr Hall has been engaged, but it doe S not lend itself really well to theatrical work and the transition to the Court Theatre is a wise one. In all likelihood the Society would have found it impossible adequately to present their latest venture, with its huge cast, on the smaller stage of the Parr Hall, and they were fortunate in being able to come to terms with the proprietors of the Royal Court Theatre. Another feature this year is the departure from the , realm of Gilbert and Sullivan. There is, of course, I nothing to surpass the work of these unique col/a- -' borateur3, but there arc other good operas and then, too, is not "variety the very spice of life"? People might, conceivably, tire even of Gilbert and Sullivan did the opera company range the whole gamut of their works before breaking fresh ground. It is perhaps merely a coincidence that in ehoosing"Merrie England." by Basil Hood and Edward German, the Society hit I upon the opera which most nearly resembles the classical productions of Gilbert and Sullivan. The two latter gentlemen were the pioneers of comic opera in this country and, in our opinion, nothing nearly approaching the high quality and unique characteristics of their work has been produced until Messrs. Hood and German collaborated in " Menlo England." This opera alone, of recent creations( possesses the brilliant word-play and tunefulness of its more renowned predecessors. Doubtless, the Society will some day I give us something quite different —such as " Les Cloches de Corneville" or" Dorothy." Meanwhile we can express complete satisfaction with " Merrie England." It gives ample scope to the members of the Society, and tlizir nightly performances have been so excellent as to fully merit the enthusiasm of very large audiences. The followinr, is the cast : _

The Earl of I....ise—s Mr. J. W. Bibby Sir Walter Raleigh M- J7Ralibitt, . .

' .. Walter Wilkinsl a Player In Shakes- }Mr. G. 11. Smith peare s c3mpauy Silas:Simkins another Player Mr. Walter Moores 1 Long NIL.. Wallace Riy Toni 1 Royal Foresters Mr. J. Seotson Big Ben . Lords Ladies, Townsfolk, &c., &c. A glance at the above is sufficient to disclose the absence of several familiar names and the presence of a number of new ones. One often hears it said that "there are as good fish in the sea as ever came out of it," and nothing could be truer. But the " sea " in this case is not limitless as the ocean, though the supply of talent entirely from the ranks of the Musical Society is certainly surprising. One would have thought the places of p!ayers like Mrs. Booth, Miss Henshall, and Mr. C. P. Gough difficult to fill from a restricted field of selection, but new- comers have stepped into the vacant places and by their own skill proved the truth of the old adage just quoted. Mr. G. H. Smith, as successor to gr. Gough is a striking case in point. Until this week he has never attempted anything so ambitious as comic opera in his life, Nand probably had no idea that he possessed the qualifications for the funny man's part which we have seen so well taken in the past by Mr. Gough. Yet the Society might look far before it found a better Walter Wilkins for "Morrie England." Mr. Smith has made a huge success of his strange role. He has the making or the marring of the whole opera in '.is hands, and the, responsibility alone might hay.:, affected a man's humour, but Mr. Smith sings and dances and frolics and fires off witticisms with a light-hearted cibandon which proves him to be equipped with both the temperament and ability for such performances as we have learnt to associate with the names of George Grossmith and Walter Passmore. His fooling in the Masque of St. George was admirably done and evoked roars of laughter, while we could not have desired a better interpretation of the always-encored "Fish song" from a man with so light a voice. Wilkins has an excellent foil in Simkins whose part is very intelligently and humorously played by Mr. Walter /foores. His make-up is picturesquely villainous,but gives him the appearance more of a criminal than a comic. This is not Mr. Moores' fault, and it might easily be remedied. Mr. Moores and Mr. Smith share equally in the popularity of the droll duet "I may lie wrong, but I love a song with a tune that a man may march to," which is so well backed up by the chorus in a rollicking refwain. Wilkins' Sousa-like contortions when he personally conducts the chorus are a triumph of mimicry and the inventive faculty. The chief soprano part of the opera is this year in charge of Miss Marie Houghton, and right well does she acquit herself. Not only does Miss Houghton make a graceful and charm- ing Bessie Throckmorton, but her singing is a delight to the ear. The dainty air "She had a letter 'from her love "—singing which she makes her entry —is one of the choicest numbers in the opera, and only the immediate incident on the stage prevents her receiving the nightly encore so well deserved. Miss Houghton has not otherwise lacked the plaudits which are her due, and she receives a most enthusiastic recognition of her brilliant vocalization in the elegant song with its exacting cadenza, "Who shall say that love is cruel." Miss Houghton is a worthy successor to Mrs. Booth, and we hope to see her frequently in local amateur opera. Two ladies who make their debut in the role of principals are Miss Emily Taylor and Miss Annie Warren, and both are to be heartily complimented. Miss Taylor makes a very attractive May Queen and her singing is fully equal to the demands of the post. A more exacting study has been demanded of Miss Warren as Jill- all-alone, the woodland maid with the ragged kirtle who comes so near being burned as a witch. She interprets cleverly the varying emotions of .Jill and sings very sweetly, particularly in the fine duet with Raleigh: "It is the merry month of May." There is some difficulty in catching the words of her songs at times, and we noted the same failing in regard to so excellent a vocalist as Miss Nellie Anderton. The latter is, of course, a contralto, and it seemed that the deeper tones of b >th voices as com- pared with the sopranos of Miss Houghton and Miss Taylor caused some loss of clearness in enunciation. Possibly the numerical strength of the orchestra contributes to this result. We have often thought that the number of musicians might be lessened with advantage to the vocalists, and especially those whose voices possess depth rather than penetration. This point has, perhaps, hardly been considered in its actual relationship to the success of the opera-as a whole. We can, and do, admire the brilliant orches- tration which the Society's musicians provide, but we should like to see it more frequently and deliberately subordinated to the demands of the moment and the vocal capacity of the artistes on the stage. One has got accustomed to looking for finished performances from Mr. J. Babbitt. He has won considerable local fame as an operatic tenor, and that fame is enhanced by his 'work in " Verne England." The part of Raleigh suits Lim perfectly and his natural grace of manner is only equalled by the delightful way in which lie sings the solos and con- certed numbers falling to his lot. Mr. Ilabbitt made the most of the well-known ballad in praise of the English Rose which opens with: Dan Cupid bath a Garden." Ur. J. W. Bibby also scores a pronounced success as the Earl of Essex and sings "Who are the yeomen, the yeomen of England," with rare power, though undoubtedly handicapped by the high key in which the song is set. He is more at ease in " When Cupid first this old world trod'" a tuneful item which also introduces concerted

passages and dainty movement for Raleigh, Bessie and Jill. Nothing but praise can be bestowed upon Miss Nellie AnderCoa who makes a most regal and handsome Queen Elizabeth. Her rage 7 hen the secret of Raleigh's real affection comes to light was very well simulated, and the song " 0 peaceful England " tunefully and sympathetically rendered. The "four worthy citizens of Windsor town " were capitally represented by Messrs. Fieldhouse (butcher), Hale (baker), Humphreys (tinker) and Hannan (tailor). They play a considerable part in the merrymaking of the opera, and the heavy wit which is intended to be characteristic of the unedu- cated tradesmen of good Queen 13039'S time is cleverly expressed. Long Tom and Big Ben are unmistakable features both of the landscape and the opera, and each plays a manly part in manly fashion. Mr. Ray made h doughty champion of Jill, and together with Bessie, Jill, and Ben took part in the excellently snag quartet "In England, merry England." The many subsidiary parts were all in really capable hands, and for uniform excellence we do not think the Society has done any- thing quite so good as " Merrie England." There is a great amount of dancing, which has, of course, entrailed very hard work in rehearsal, and the result is a credit to both the artistes and their " coach." Miss Marjory Dodds, Miss Boyle, Miss D. Truslove, , and Miss Marie Warren lead the way in many move- ments, and separately distinguish themselves in the sinuous Egyptian Dance near the close. The work of the chorus was particularly well clone ; the singing affording just the necessary support to the soloists,and yet never lacking volume. Quite a feature of the opera was the spectacular effect produc9cl by skilful grouping, and here too the value of a well-trained chorus was manifest. If anything, there was rather too large a company on the stage at times, and a redaction in numbers would have brought the ton.t ensemble even nearer to perfection. We need hardly say that the orchestra acquitted itself with honour. It always does so, and we should I only feel disposed to refer in detail to the band-playing in the event of its falling below the customary high standard. Mr. Crossley's onerous task as conductor was performed with marked , skill, and not a little of tho " finish" which was I apparent in so many directions was due to his pains- taking care. The bulk of Mr. _Peden Williams' work has, naturally, been performed prior to the opening night, but there is abundant evidence of the expert knowledge he has brought to bear in getting his company part-perfect. He should be proud of the excellent all-round results and of this week's signal triumph. At the commencement of the second Act j Mr. W. H. Payton, 11113. Bee., took skilful charge of I the chorus heard " off " the stage, and during the arduous and prolonged rehearsals he acted also as the Opera Company's accompanist. Acknowledgement should be made of the fact that Messrs. W. E. Atkin and Lionel FairClough acted as prompters, although, happily their sorvices never seem to have been required. The special scenery depicting Windsor Castle and the Thames was pro- vided by Messrs. F. le Maistre and Co., of Man- chester, the costomes. wigs, and properties by Messrs. B. J. Simmons and Co. of London, special shoes for all the ladies were made by the Public Benefit Boot Co. Warrington, and the very necessary " make-up ' ' services were artistically rendered by Messrs. Birch Browne and Son Warrington, and Messrs. B. J. Simmons and Co., London. s• CROSFIELDS' BAND.

COMPLIMENTARY DINNER AT THE WORKS. Speech By Mr. Crosfield, M.P.

Another instance of Mr. A. H. Crosfield's generosity was exemplified last Saturday evening. This time his hospitality was extended to the Crosfield Prize Band. The merribers of the band were accompanied, in some cases by their wives, in others by their sweethearts. Two happy couples took along with them their infants. At one point in the evening, one of the infants caused much laughter, for while fr. J. iV. Willett was singing "Good-bye my Darling ! " the baby cried out "Daddy! " The dinner which Mr. Crosfield gave was partaken of in Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., large dining room, and was enjoyed very much by those present. Supporting Mr. Crosfield were his wife, Miss Elliadi (Mrs. Crosfleld's sister, Miss Truda Crosfleld, Mrs. Hamilton, Ciptain G. Crosfield, Lieutenant Owen and Mr. Hamilton.

After dinner an excellent programme was gone through the following ladies and gentlemen taking part: Mrs. Crosfield. Miss Elliadi, Miss Barnas, Ciptain Crosfleld, Mr. J. W. Willett, Mr. Pitts, Mr. W. W. Whittle and Mr. A. Allinson. Messrs. Winstanley, Bates, Harrison, and Bedforth (Bandmaster), formed the Instrumental Quartette Party. Mr. J. Foster acted as accompanist. Mr. WILLETT moved a vote of thanks to Mr. A. H. Ccosfleld, M.P., for presiding and in the course of his remarks said that Mr. Crosfield had been heart and soul with the band ever since it was formed.

This was received with the singing of "For he is a jolley good fellow!" Mr. CROSFIELD, in responding to Mr. Willett's remarks said he thanked everybody there, on behalf of his wife and on his own account, for the great kindness with which they had received the few remarks from his friend Mr. Willett. 'At the same time he was bound to say that Mr. Willett had dis- tinctly infringed the understanding and compact entered into a little earlier in the evening. He feared that the example set by people in very high places of deviating from the obligations of compacts and under- standings might be catching. He was not sure whether their friend Mr. Willett had taken a hand in the little game that had re3ently been played in that part of the near east known as the Balkans,—(laughter)—but if he had not helped to break the concert of Europe he had certainly infringed the concert of Bank Quay. (More laughter.) He would not continue what they might after all think a little out of place, namely, jocular allusions to the serious and somewhat cynical events in the near East, which had occupied every one's mind recently. Those rather ominous proceedings might have far-reaching consequences, but they could only hope that wise statesmanship might still be successful in finding a satisfactory solution of a very complex and difficult situation. (Hear, hear.) Well, he would spare Mr. Willett's feelings and say nothing liurther of this little incident in which, by taking French leave— (laughter)—he had let them in for a little super- fluous speech-making, when they would all have been much more pleasantly engaged in listening to such charming music. However, under the circumstances ho was heartily glad of the chance to offer the band in the name of everyone present unstinted congratulations on their really magnificent performance, during the past twelve months. (iiear, hear). They had won victory' after victory, and alto,gether had been successful on 22, different occasions, while their achievement at the Crystal Palace was indeed, what old Oliver Cromwell might have described as a "crowning mercy." (Laughter and cheers). He thought that without any- thing in the nature of vain glory, everyone connected with that great industrial centre at Bank Quay, in whatever position either on the commercial or the manufacturing side of the works, was entitled to feel very proud of the band's splendid achievement. (Cheers.) They had now a splendid record to live up to, and he hoped they would succeed in going even one better in the future. (Reaewed cheers.) All the directors and all those responsible for the conduct of the firm's affairs, felt the deepest satisfaction at the success of the social development in connection with their business in recent years. That development was an admirable thing in every way, and beneficial to all concerned, and indirectly, therefore, to the firm itself. While the social development had extended in many directions one of the most signal successes had been the various forms of musical culture, and that pleasant informal gathering that night was, as they all knew, arranged to celebrate the band's great victory. (Cheers.) There would be another occasion shortly on which proper tribute would be paid to the band ; but that evening all of those present were particularly glad to have an opportunity of themselves personally congratu- lating, both individually and collectively,every member of the band on their famous victory. (Loud cheers). Captain CROSFIELD said he hardly thought that the Chairman in his remarks had laid it on quite thick enough. (Laughter.) But, like the Chairman, he attached the highest importance to this signal victory, and the directors would hope to see the band achieving as great, or better still, even greater success at the Crystal Palace next year. (Loud cheers.) Going back two or three hundred years, Captain Crosfield said they had no bands, and what was the result of the condition of things which obtained then and much more recently too ? Life in the villages was so dull that the people flocked to the towns and now, at the present Jay, there was eighty per cent, of our population living in the towns. Let them increase the attractions of the country districts and we should then maintain our agricultural population. (Cheers.) He took a great interest in music himself, and attached very great importance to the musical development that was taking place in the country. He did not believe in in a mournful, depressing class of music, but on the contrary he hoped that in the coming winter their splendid Band, with a more inspiriting kind of music, would help to create and stimulate a patriotic spirit, not only in Warrington but throughout Lancashire. They could be an immense help, and follow- ing them speakers would rouse the patriotism of their audiences. (Applause.) They must not fotget the men who did the work ; they must not forget the Secretary (Ir. Taylor). He knew what being a secretary was—it was a terror. They must not forget the valuable services rendered by Mr. Halliwell. (Applause.) He concluding by hoping they would win the trophy next year. I Mr. TAYLOR and Mr. HaLtawELL briefly responded.

THE NOVEMBER ELECTIONS. Saturday next is the last day for receiving nomina- tions of candidates for the November elections and interest in the two contests already assured has increased considerably during the past week. In Orford Ward there is a keen struggle between Mr. Peter Peacock (L.), Mr. J. Hampson (S.), and Mr. J. A. Rushworth (C.) In his address to the electors Mr. Peacock strongly urged the necessity for the completion of the - I original scheme for the Orford Ward recreation 4 ground, including the laying of bowling greens, open-air baths, &c. He will, if elected, again I press for the introduction of cl. fa-es on the * Longford tram route which he advocated unsuc- cessfully during his previous term of office. With regard to the Old Age Pensions Act, Mr. Peacock as an active worker in connection with various Friendly Societies, expresses astonishment at the action of the Town Council in declining to co-opt representatives . from trade unions and friendly societies to serve on. the Distribution Committee. If elected, he will at the first meeting of the Council move a resolution of such • a nature, that if carried will immediately remedy this . defect, and cause a committee to be formed which will comply with the spirit of the Act. He believes the 1 Education Act as administered to-day: in Warrington, is giving general satisfaction and he is therefore not in favour of any alteration. In St. John's Ward Councillor James Ball (S), the retiring representative, is being opposed by Mr. Frederick T. Lashford, butcher, Chorley-street. The Conservatives are putting forth every effort in St. John's and Orford Wards and imperial questions figure prominently in their speeches. They are also endeavour- ing to prejudice Councillor Ball's candidature by quotations from the writings of Robert Blatchford on 'Christianity. There was a lively scene at their meeting on Thursday evening, their refusal to allow questions to be asked being vigorously resented by a number of Socialists present who later proceeded to address the crowd on their own account. It is stated that Councillor W. Benson (N.T.) does not intend to seek re-election for Town Hall Ward. Mr. T. Birtles is receiving hearty support from all sections of the community. There is a rumour that Mr. W. J. Shaw. licensed victualler, intends to oppose Councillor Dickenson (N.T.) in Bewsey Ward. The following is a list of prospective candidates :--• A Town Hall Ward hr. THE SOCIALIST CANDIISATES.

The National Union of Gas-workers and General Labourers at a Warrington branch meeting held on October 11th passed the following resolution unanimously :—" Seeing that two of our members 1 Bro J. Ball and Bro. J. Hampson are contesting the St. John's and Orford Wards respectively at the forth- coming November elections, this branch heartily endorses their candidature and pledges itself to actively support them." COUNCILLOR BALL'S NOVEL SUGGESTION. Councillor Ball has addressed a letter to his opponent, Mr. Last:ford, dated October 15th, ic which he says : "It has been suggested that you and I hold a joint 1, public indoor meeting in the Ward under an impartial ' Chairman, to be previously appointed, and that your party and mine each pay half the expense. lam quite willing to debate municipal matters with you on a public platform and trust you will see your way to agree to this suggestion of the electors. If for any reason you do not care to adopt this course I shall be pleased to meet either of your colleagues, W. Peter Rylands, Esq., or Roger Parr, Esq., on mmilar terms, its their views on municipal matters would no doubt educate the eloctors. I trust to receive an early and favourable reply."

FOR THE BILL. Monday Evening's Temperance Demonstration.

The second great meeting in support of the Govern- ment Licensing Bill, organised by the Warrington Citizen's Temperance League, will be held in Parr Hall on Monday evening. A most attractive platform of speakers has been secured, including Principal Warman, M.A., B.D. (Liverpool), M. J. A.Seddon, M.P.,Rev. A. J. Palmer (London), and Bev. F. A. Serbeton, M.A., Pf Hyde (formerly vicar of Christ Church, Latchford). The chair will be occupied by the Dean of Carlisle (Canon Barker). The public is requested to note that the meeting will commence at seven o'clock prompts doors being opened at 6 p.m.

Kingswood, Frodsham.

It was the harvest festival at Kingswood Primitivp et Methodist Chapel last Sunday and Mondaf. The pulpit was occupied on Sunday by Mr. Whitley, of Stockton Heath, an old time favourite on these occasions ?.t, Kingswood. Mr. Whitleys first appearance in the pulpit since his illness some months ago was well appreciated by frill congregations, chairs and forms having to be. borrowed from the neighbourhood, to accommodate the evening gathering. The choir rendered their part very creditably in special harvest anthems under the leadership of the Rev. J. H. Rolla.son (circuit minister); in the afternoon, and Mr. Joho Hayes in the evening. There was a large congregation assembled oa the Monday to hoar Mr. Whitley give a resura of the past fifty years—which was much appreciate& for its varied outlook through this length of, time. Mr. Whitley had for his chairman Mr. Fryer, of Newton, one of his chairmen of years ago on n similar occasion at Kingswood, and one who could rejoice with him in almost half a century of Christian service. If the value of their services may be judged, by the collection there were certainly many thankful hearts.

_ Mr. Ellison afterwards took the leading part in arranging and disposing of the splendid show of fruit/ and vegetables, so ably and beautifully arranged by Mr. John Spruce and his numerous helpers, including that: old and most respected member Mrs. Summerflold. The total proceeds are slightly in advance of th past four years' results, Local Farmer Shoots Himself.

____ --....—... An inquest was held by Mr. Thos. Ridgway at Higher Whitley, near Warrington, yesterday, on th body of Alfred Barber, 55 years of age, of the 014 Mill Farm, Higher Whitley, who committed suicide 0 Thursday morning by shooting himself with a gun The evidence showed that the deceased got up ? Thursday morning about five o'clock, and told his Wl° he was going to light a fire and call the boys. Shortl : afterwards Mrs. Barber and her son went to look fo the deceased, and they found him in the Dutch barr) with a double-barrelled gun lying at his feet. There: was one empty cartridgo in the right barrel, deceased had shot himself just below the heart. 01510 I) wife asked him if he had intended to shoot hinar . when he got up, and he replied in the negativet adding that he was sorry for what he had done. 14* doctor was summoned, but the deceased died ahou 7.30 the same morning. Deceased had not been for some weeks and could not sleep well. A. verchc of " Suicide whilst of unsound mind " was returned'

COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER. NroucH IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next .1.1 GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER will be holden at the COURT-HOUSE, KNUTSFORD, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, the 19th day of OCTOBER, 1908, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Altrincham, Bucklow, Dukinfield, Middlewich, Sandbach, Congleton, Northwich, Prestbury and Stockport, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from such Divisions, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the COURT HOUSE, KNUTSFORD, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 19th day of OCTOBER NEXT, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COURT will be Adjourned to the CASTLE OF CHESTER on WEDNESDAY the 28th day of OCTOBER, 1908, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when another Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Broxton, Chester Castle, Daresbury, Eddisbury, Nantwich, Runcorn, Winsford and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last- mentioned Divisions will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, at the Opening of the Court on WEDNES- DAY, the 28th day of OCTOBER, 1008, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenon. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County Palatine of Chester. Chester, 28th September, 1908.
