In many cases, when animals are ill-treated, it is done unintentionally, for any one who in- flicts pain on an animal for the sake of hurting it, and finds pleasure in so doing, must, in- , deed, have taken leave of any sense of feeling. At home I have a book with some hints ort kindness to animals. This is the one that heads the list:— "Everyone intending to keep animals should first learn what is requisite for the health and happiness of each, and, if unable to provide it a suitable home, a sufficient and punctual supply of wholesome food, and opportunities for healthy exercise, should at once decide nog to keep it." If only this rule was obeyed, many animals bought on the spur of the mcnzent, their owners—generally children—having none, or a very inadequate, idea of what is requisite for their food, or accommodation for them, but foolishly thinking that it would be time to see to such things when the pet was obtained, would be saved discomfort, pain, and not sel- dom death, for it is while the owner—the novelty of his new possession worn off—is leisurely inquiring as to the habite and proper food of his pet, that it suffers, maybe cooped up in a much too small box, for want of air, light, or food. So adhere to this rule, and you will be right. instead of thinking it will be time to become acquainted with its proper food, and procure a home for it, when you get your pet, reverse it, and do not think of the pet until these mat- ters ire ascertained. Most animals are grateful, kindness to them is not wasted; let them hear your voice when a tit-bit is given, and soon they will come run- ning to you when called, and although you have found the way to their feelings "through their stomachs," as the saying goes, yet they are none the less sincere for that. Horses, it may be noted, if treated kindly, invariably work in a more wholehearted way than those of their kind who are ill-treated, su to treat them kindly pays their master. Most cruelty, if it is not as I have already said, done unintentionally, it is thoughtlesslYr and a word to the offenders will often stop it. Let us decide with Wordsworth:— 'Never to blend our pleasure or our pride, With sorrow of the meanest thing that feels?' HITEEP.T. H. HAyES.

CHRYSANTHEMUM SHOW AT STOCKTON HEATH. Opening by the Mayoress. -- There was a flee display of chrysanthemums on Satur- day when the Stockton Heath Garden Society held their fourth annual show of these beautiful flowers in the Church Institute, Victoria-square. In addition to the chrysanthemums there were also other autumn flowers, and the whole formed a- pleasing collection. The increase in exnibits was most marked, there being 170 as compared with 75 last year. It is also pleasing to note that 40 children competed in the exhibition. The judges (Messrs. Poulton and Wakefield) had great difficulty in placing the winning cards, owing to the general excellence of the flowers. THE OPENING CEREMONY. The Mayoress of Warrington, who was accompanied I by the Mayor, opened the show. Mr. Vere Davies (President of the society),in a short address, referred to the excellent way in which both the IMayor a.id Mayoress had carried out their official duties during the past two years, adding that the only regret was that they were about to relinquish the office. He I thought the chrysanthemum was a very interesting flower. They were all proud of their national emblem, the rose, but if he were a Japanese he would be equally , proud of the chrysanthemum. They had an excellent I show of that class of flower. He, however, fai led to j notice any single flower chrysanthemums in competi- tion. They were very difficult to grow, but would form a very interesting class, and next year he hoped to see some exhibited. (Applause). The Mayoress said she was proud of the task of open- ing the show. They in Stockton Heath were noted for their flower shows, their gardens and trees. They kept improving, and she hoped that they would continue in that course, and that Warrington would follow their example. She had great pleasure in declaring the show open. (Applause). Mr. Plinston proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayoress, which was seconded by Mr. Evans. Miss Timperley then presented the Mayoress with a pretty bouquet frcm the Society. The Mayor said he was pleased to find the juveniles of Stockton Heath were taking an interest in garden- ing. The baskets of autumn foliage were a feast for the eyes and a credit to the district. He admired Stockton Heath because the people were trying to make it a place of beauty and a joy for ever. (Applause.) Mr. McDermid proposed a vote of thanks to the judges whom he complimented upon the way they had carried out their duties. This was seconded by Mr. B. Bradshaw and was heartily passed. Mr. Wakefield in reply said Mr. Poulton and he had been surprised to find so many beautiful chrysan- themums so early in the seasog. The classes were well filled and the Bogie,'' was excellent. Ile congratulated the committee upon having so many competitors taking an interest in the cultivation of chrysanthemums. (Applause.) A vote of thanks was accorded to the president for presiding on the propositicn of Mr. Willott, seccnled by the Rev. J. Collins. PRIZE LIST.
